353 — HPC Job Scheduler (Slurm-like) from Scratch

Project Number: 353

Project Name: HPC Job Scheduler (Slurm-like) from Scratch

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 Precise Engineering Scope

Build a production-grade, Slurm-like HPC batch scheduler from scratch in Rust: a multi-node control plane consisting of a central controller daemon (hpcsched-controld) and per-node executor daemons (hpcsched-noded). Users submit resource-typed jobs (nodes, cpus-per-task, memory, GPUs, time-limit, partition, QoS, account); the controller queues them, computes multifactor + fair-share priority, allocates physical resources via core/GPU/mem bitmaps, dispatches job steps to node daemons, tracks accounting, and survives controller restart (durable WAL + checkpoint) and node failure (requeue or fail affected jobs).

Language rationale (locked): A cluster resource manager is a long-lived control plane that must never double-allocate a node, never lose a committed job across a restart, and survive node failures without crashing. Rust provides a strong job/state-machine type system (exhaustive enum dispatch, borrow-checking prevent concurrent mutation of allocation bitmaps), durable-state correctness (no GC pauses during WAL append), and a robust async RPC story via Tokio for the controller-to-node-daemon control plane.

1.2 System Boundaries

Boundary In scope Out of scope
Scheduler submit, queue, prioritize, allocate, dispatch, account, HA intra-job parallel runtime (project 352)
Resource types CPU cores, memory, GPU devices, wall-clock time (TRES) network topology beyond feature-matching, storage quotas (deferred)
Fault handling node heartbeat timeout → requeue/fail; controller failover job checkpoint/restart (application-level, deferred)
Scale target thousands of nodes, 10 k+ pending jobs petascale burst-buffer / storage scheduler (project 354)
Interface CLI binaries (sbatch/srun/squeue/scancel/sinfo/sacct analogs) web UI, REST API (deferred)
Security Unix user identity at submit; account/QoS association checks Kerberos / MUNGE authentication (deferred)

1.3 Non-Goals (explicit)

  • PhaseDeferred(D1) — multi-site federation / cross-cluster meta-scheduling.
  • PhaseDeferred(D2) — live job checkpoint to distributed storage.
  • PhaseDeferred(D3) — REST/gRPC external API for cloud bursting.
  • PhaseDeferred(D4) — MUNGE/Kerberos wire-level authentication.
  • PhaseDeferred(D5) — network-topology-aware placement beyond node feature matching.
  • PhaseDeferred(D6) — burst-buffer and parallel filesystem accounting.

1.4 Intended Deployment Model

Head node:   hpcsched-controld (primary) + standby replica (Phase 12)
             shared durable store: local NVMe WAL + checkpoint (single-site) or
             shared POSIX filesystem / replicated volume (HA mode)
Compute nodes: hpcsched-noded on every schedulable host
Admin/users: hpcsched-cli binaries (sbatch, srun, squeue, scancel, sinfo, sacct)
             communicating with controld via TCP control-plane RPC

Target environment: on-premise HPC cluster, 8–4096 compute nodes, homogeneous or heterogeneous hardware (CPU-only + GPU nodes in separate partitions). Single administrative domain.

1.5 Target Users and Operators

Role Primary interface Needs
HPC user sbatch, srun, squeue, scancel submit batch/interactive jobs; query status; cancel
HPC operator sinfo, controld config, drain/down node control monitor cluster; manage partitions; handle node faults
Account manager sacct, association/QoS config enforce allocations; audit TRES usage; adjust fair-share shares
Systems developer (this project) crate API + test harness extend scheduler; add resource types; integrate site plugins

1.6 Phase 0 Deliverables — Locked Crates

Ordered by dependency; all eleven ship as a Cargo workspace:

  1. hpcsched-core — Job, JobId, JobState, ResourceSpec, Node, NodeId, Partition; pure model; no I/O.
  2. hpcsched-proto — control-plane wire protocol: submit, alloc, launch, heartbeat, status, cancel messages.
  3. hpcsched-queue — pending queue, partitions, priority-ordered views, eligible-set computation.
  4. hpcsched-alloc — resource allocator: node/core/GPU/mem bitmaps, topology-aware selection.
  5. hpcsched-sched — scheduling engine: main priority pass + EASY backfill + reservations.
  6. hpcsched-fairshare — hierarchical fair-share tree + multifactor priority (age, QoS, size, fairshare).
  7. hpcsched-acct — accounting: job records, TRES usage, association/QoS limit enforcement.
  8. hpcsched-state — durable controller state: WAL + periodic checkpoint + replay.
  9. hpcsched-controld — controller daemon binary: scheduling loop, authoritative state, RPC server.
  10. hpcsched-noded — node daemon binary: launch/monitor job steps, report + enforce resources via cgroup.
  11. hpcsched-cli — sbatch / srun / squeue / scancel / sinfo / sacct analog binaries.

1.7 What Later Phases Unlock

Phases Capability unlocked
1–2 Node inventory, daemon registration, heartbeat liveness
3 Job submission, pending queue, WAL durability
4 Resource allocation (core/GPU/mem bitmaps)
5 Priority scheduling loop; Pending→Running transitions
6 Job step launch, process supervision, lifecycle epilog
7 EASY backfill; backfill efficiency metrics
8 Multifactor priority; fair-share decay; account tree
9 Preemption, gang scheduling, QoS tiers
10 Node fault handling; drain/down; requeue on node loss
11 TRES accounting; limit enforcement; sacct queries
12 Controller HA; standby failover; fencing
13 Full CLI suite; Prometheus metrics; operational dashboards
14 Scale/soak/conformance; 10 k-job scheduling-pass latency target met

1.8 Estimated Timeline

Scope Duration
Phase 0 (crate scaffolding + core model) 1–2 weeks
Phases 1–6 (functional scheduler, no backfill/fairshare) 8–14 weeks
Phases 7–11 (full feature set) 10–16 weeks
Phases 12–14 (HA, CLI, scale/soak) 6–10 weeks
Full project ~6–10 months

1.9 Prerequisites

Domain Specific knowledge required
OS internals Linux cgroups v2 (cpu, memory, devices controllers); process supervision; signal handling (SIGTERM/SIGKILL epilog)
Distributed systems WAL design; epoch-based failover; split-brain fencing; heartbeat liveness detection
Async Rust Tokio task model; select!; cancellation; backpressure in RPC pipelines
Scheduling theory EASY backfill algorithm; conservative vs. aggressive backfill tradeoffs; bin-packing NP-hardness
HPC operations Slurm concepts (partition, QoS, association, TRES, GRES); workload patterns (MPI strong-scaling, GPU training)
Bitmaps / combinatorics Fixed bitset allocation; fragmentation metrics; topology-aware selection heuristics

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge

Topic What the implementation teaches
Resource allocation as transaction Bitmap-level double-allocation prevention; read-modify-write under concurrent submission
WAL + checkpoint + replay Exactly-once state reconstruction; WAL compaction; idempotent event replay
Backfill scheduling EASY algorithm correctness; reservation management; time-estimation sensitivity
Hierarchical fair-share Decayed usage propagation up an association tree; weight tuning effects on fairness
Multifactor priority Composing orthogonal factors (age, QoS, fairshare, size) into a monotonic scalar
Preemption Suspend vs. requeue semantics; grace period enforcement; priority inversion avoidance
Node daemon supervision Cgroup resource enforcement; heartbeat timeout detection; partial-failure handling
Controller HA Standby replay lag; failover atomicity; fencing protocol correctness
Scheduling-pass performance Priority-queue data structures at 10 k+ job scale; backfill depth limiting

2.2 Historical and Architectural Significance

  • Slurm (Yoo, Jette, Grondona, JSSPP 2003) established the slurmctld/slurmd split-daemon model; nearly every modern HPC cluster runs it or a descendant.
  • EASY backfill (Lifka 1995, Mu'alem & Feitelson 2001) remains the dominant utilization technique for batch systems; building it from scratch forces deep understanding of reservation windows and time-estimation contracts.
  • Fair-share scheduling addresses the Nash equilibrium problem of shared cluster ownership: without decay, early users permanently starve late ones.
  • The controller-as-single-source-of-truth pattern (P2) is the architectural ancestor of Borg, Kubernetes kube-scheduler, and YARN ResourceManager — understanding it at the bitmap level provides a foundation for all modern cluster managers.
  • HPC schedulers are among the oldest production distributed systems still in active research; they expose every classic distributed systems problem: clock skew, partial failure, split-brain, leader election, and state machine replication.

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

Muscle Exercise mechanism
Type-state machine design JobState enum with exhaustive transition guards (I3, I7)
Concurrent data structure ownership CoreBitmap as a per-node transactional unit under Tokio; P3
Fault classification Distinguish transient node loss from permanent failure; requeue policy (P7)
Deterministic testing of scheduling logic P8: scheduling pass is a pure function; property-based testing
Bounded algorithm design Backfill depth limit; scheduling-pass time budget; P9
Observability engineering Prometheus counters, histograms, and gauges wired to every scheduling decision
Protocol design Binary wire format for high-frequency heartbeat + low-frequency control messages

2.4 Production Concepts Exercised

  • WAL before ack (P6): transactional durability without a database.
  • Crash-only design (P10): no shutdown protocol; replay is the recovery path.
  • Cgroup v2 resource enforcement: noded-side budget enforcement independent of scheduler-side accounting.
  • Fencing: prevent old controller from allocating after failover (I9).
  • Drain semantics: graceful node maintenance without disrupting running work (I6).
  • Epilog/prolog hooks: site-specific job setup/teardown inside noded, isolated from scheduler logic.

2.5 Cross-Links to Related Portfolio Projects

Project Relationship
352 — Intra-node parallel runtime The runtime that job steps execute; this project dispatches to it
354 — Distributed compute engine Upper-layer workload that submits jobs to this scheduler
Linux kernel / cgroup project Cgroup v2 interface used by hpcsched-noded for resource enforcement
Distributed key-value store WAL + checkpoint pattern mirrors storage engine durability design
Raft / consensus library Controller HA (Phase 12) could optionally replace bespoke failover with a consensus log
Async RPC / networking project Control-plane protocol in hpcsched-proto exercises the same Tokio patterns
Database query executor Scheduling-pass as a plan over a priority-indexed relation; optimizer analogs

2.6 Calibration Value

A candidate who has implemented this project demonstrates: correctness-first thinking under shared mutable resource constraints; operational awareness (drain, fencing, requeue policies); algorithm-level knowledge of backfill and fair-share; and the ability to make a distributed system durable and observable. This places the implementer at the level of mid-to-senior HPC infrastructure engineer or systems software engineer working on cluster management at large compute providers.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

  • Phase 3–4 (WAL + bitmap): Can explain why fdatasync before ack is the minimal correctness requirement, describe the ARIES force/steal taxonomy, and derive the correct WAL record ordering to prevent double-allocation across a crash boundary — without referencing external material.
  • Phase 5–7 (scheduling + EASY backfill): Can reproduce the EASY backfill correctness proof from first principles, identify the regime where conservative backfill (per-job reservations) is strictly better than EASY (large clusters with tight SLAs), and diagnose a priority-inversion bug by replaying a WAL snapshot.
  • Phase 8 (fair-share): Can derive the multifactor priority formula from the Nash equilibrium framing of shared cluster ownership, select a decay half-life for a given fairness vs. recovery-speed tradeoff, and detect gaming via short-job bursting from a sacct trace.
  • Phase 9 (preemption + QoS): Can design a preemption tier hierarchy for a mixed HPC/AI workload, explain why Suspend is unsafe for MPI jobs, and write the invariant that prevents a preemption loop between two equal-priority QoS tiers.
  • Phase 10 (node fault): Can describe the split-brain scenario between heartbeat loss and actual node death, choose the missed-heartbeat threshold for a given blast-radius vs. detection-latency tradeoff, and identify when force-free of bitmaps violates I1 without the fencing guard.
  • Phase 12 (HA failover): Can specify the minimal fence protocol that upholds I9 with a POSIX-only shared filesystem (no etcd), prove correctness under two simultaneous standbys, and size the lease TTL for a given NFS fsync latency distribution.
  • Phase 14 (scale/conformance): Can predict scheduling-pass latency from cluster size, queue depth, and backfill depth using the capacity model, identify the O(N²) path before profiling, and write a conformance fixture that would have caught the Slurm sched/backfill reservation-horizon bug (CVE-style regression).

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

        users: sbatch / srun / squeue / scancel / sinfo / sacct   (hpcsched-cli)
                                   |
                                   v   control-plane RPC (hpcsched-proto)
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
  |                  Controller daemon (hpcsched-controld)                  |
  |  +-----------------+   +------------------+   +----------------------+   |
  |  | Submission +    |   | Scheduling loop  |   | Allocator            |   |
  |  | pending queue   |-->| main pass        |-->| node/core/GPU/mem    |   |
  |  | (hpcsched-queue)|   | (priority) +     |   | bitmaps + topology   |   |
  |  +-----------------+   | EASY backfill    |   | (hpcsched-alloc)     |   |
  |  +-----------------+   | (hpcsched-sched) |   +----------------------+   |
  |  | Fair-share tree |   +------------------+   +----------------------+   |
  |  | + multifactor   |   | Durable state    |   | Accounting / TRES /  |   |
  |  | (hpcsched-      |   | WAL + checkpoint |   | limits (hpcsched-acct)|  |
  |  |  fairshare)     |   | (hpcsched-state) |   +----------------------+   |
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
        |  launch step (RPC)                       ^  heartbeat + resource report
        v                                          |
  +--------------+   +--------------+   +--------------+      ... compute nodes
  | noded N0     |   | noded N1     |   | noded N2     |
  | launch +     |   |              |   |              |
  | monitor job  |   |              |   |              |
  | steps; cgroup|   |              |   |              |
  | resource     |   |              |   |              |
  | enforcement; |   |              |   |              |
  | report state |   |              |   |              |
  +--------------+   +--------------+   +--------------+

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

hpcsched-cli (user-facing binaries). Trust boundary: untrusted user space; all commands authenticate by Unix UID passed in the submit RPC. Data flow out: submit RPC (sbatch/srun), cancel RPC (scancel), query RPCs (squeue/sinfo/sacct). Data flow in: status responses, job IDs, tabular output. No persistent state; purely a stateless RPC client.

Control-plane RPC (hpcsched-proto). Defines the wire protocol between CLI, controld, and noded. Message types: SubmitReq/Resp, AllocNotify, LaunchMsg, HeartbeatMsg, StepStatusMsg, CancelReq, StatusQuery/Resp. Transport: length-prefixed binary frames over TCP (Tokio async I/O). Trust boundary: controld authenticates noded connections by node hostname + shared secret; CLI connections authenticated by UID. Concurrency boundary: each connection is a Tokio task; messages serialized per-connection; controld's event loop serializes mutations.

Submission + pending queue (hpcsched-queue). Receives validated SubmitReq; constructs Job with initial Pending state; appends JobSubmitted WAL record before acking; inserts into a priority-ordered pending queue (BinaryHeap or sorted skip-list keyed by PriorityFactors). Exposes: eligible_set(partition, node_inventory) — jobs that can in principle run given current resource inventory and partition constraints. Data flow in: submit RPC. Data flow out: ordered job view to hpcsched-sched; priority updates from hpcsched-fairshare.

Fair-share tree + multifactor priority (hpcsched-fairshare). Maintains the Account/Association tree: each node holds shares, usage_decayed, children. On each scheduling pass (and periodically in background): recomputes fair-share factor per user/account; assembles PriorityFactors { age, fairshare, partition, qos, job_size, weights } → scalar u32 priority. Decay: half-life configurable; usage flows from leaf (user) to root (cluster). No I/O; pure in-memory computation seeded from hpcsched-acct usage records. Trust boundary: internal; not directly reachable by users.

Scheduling loop (hpcsched-sched). Core decision engine. Runs on a timer tick (configurable interval, default 0.5–2 s) or on state-change event. Main pass: iterate eligible pending jobs in priority order; call hpcsched-alloc for each; on success transition Pending→Configuring, append JobAllocated WAL, send LaunchMsg to noded. EASY backfill pass: compute Reservation for the top pending job; iterate lower-priority eligible jobs; allocate those whose projected end ≤ reservation start. Invariant P4, I4. Depth-limited: at most backfill_depth (default 500) candidates examined per pass (P9). Concurrency: single-threaded within the pass; no re-entrant scheduling.

Allocator (hpcsched-alloc). Operates on per-Node CoreBitmap, GpuSet, and alloc_mem. allocate(spec, node_inventory) → Option<Allocation>: selects a set of nodes satisfying ResourceSpec (feature constraints, count, contiguity if requested); atomically sets bits transactionally (read-copy-update over the bitmap); returns Allocation { job_id, nodes: Vec<(NodeId, CoreBitmap, GpuSet, mem)> }. free(allocation): clears bits on job completion. Invariants I1, I2, I3. No async I/O; pure in-memory; called synchronously inside the scheduling loop.

Durable state (hpcsched-state). WAL: append-only log of state-change events (JobSubmitted, JobAllocated, JobCompleted, NodeDown, ...) written to disk before any acknowledgement (P6). Checkpoint: periodic full snapshot of controller state; WAL truncated post-checkpoint. Replay: on startup, load latest checkpoint, replay WAL records from that point; produces exact prior state (I5). Used by HA standby (Phase 12) to tail WAL and track primary state. Persistence boundary: only component that touches disk in the controller process.

Accounting / TRES / limits (hpcsched-acct). Records per-job TRES usage (cpu-seconds, mem-seconds, gpu-seconds) on job completion and periodically for running jobs. Enforces association/QoS limits at submit time (max jobs in flight, max TRES, GrpTRES) and at schedule time (remaining quota check). Feeds hpcsched-fairshare with decayed usage. Serves sacct query RPC. Data flow in: JobCompleted events + periodic running-job polls. Data flow out: usage records to fairshare; limit verdicts to submission path; tabular data to sacct CLI.

Node daemons (hpcsched-noded). One process per compute node; no cluster-wide state. On startup: registers with controld (node id, cpu/mem/gpu/features). Periodic heartbeat: HeartbeatMsg { node_id, timestamp, free_cpus, free_mem, free_gpus, running_steps }. On LaunchMsg: spawns job step processes under cgroup v2 hierarchy (cpu quota, memory limit, device allowlist for GPUs); monitors via waitpid + resource sampling. On step exit: sends StepStatusMsg { step_id, exit_code, rusage } → controld advances job FSM. On CancelMsg: sends SIGTERM then SIGKILL after grace period; cleans up cgroup. Trust boundary: compute node; not in user trust domain; controld sends signed launch tokens (Phase 2+). Concurrency: one Tokio task per running step plus one heartbeat task.

4. System Constraints

4.1 Quantified Engineering Targets

Dimension Target Notes
Cluster scale 4 096 compute nodes, 10 k+ pending jobs design target; not a hard ceiling
Scheduling-pass latency p50 ≤ 200 ms for 10 k pending jobs, 4 k nodes (P9)
Scheduling-pass latency p99 ≤ 800 ms under peak churn (submit + cancel storm)
Backfill-pass latency p99 ≤ 500 ms depth-limited to 500 candidates (P9)
Submit RPC latency p99 ≤ 50 ms WAL fsync included; from CLI send to ack
Heartbeat processing rate ≥ 4 096 heartbeats / interval 30 s default interval; ≤ 10 ms per heartbeat event in loop
Heartbeat interval 30 s default, 10 s configurable minimum noded → controld
Node failure detection latency ≤ 3 × heartbeat interval (default 90 s) missed-heartbeat threshold = 3
Job requeue latency post-node-down ≤ 5 s after node declared Down from Down decision to Requeued state
Controller restart + replay time ≤ 30 s for 100 k WAL records checkpoint every 5 k events target
Failover RTO (HA mode) ≤ 60 s standby tail lag + fencing + promotion
WAL write amplification ≤ 2× (one write + one fsync per event) no double-logging
Checkpoint size ≤ 50 MB for 10 k jobs + 4 k nodes binary-encoded state snapshot
Controller RSS ≤ 2 GB at 10 k pending + 4 k nodes in-memory bitmaps + queue + acct records
noded RSS per node ≤ 64 MB cgroup management + per-step metadata
CoreBitmap width up to 1 024 bits (1 024-core node) u64 array, 16 words
GpuSet width up to 64 bits (64 GPU node) u64
Max concurrent RPC connections (controld) 8 192 Tokio accept loop; one task per connection
Max concurrent running job steps per node 256 noded cgroup tree depth limit
Allocation transaction time ≤ 1 µs per node evaluated bitmap AND + popcount; pure memory
Priority recompute time ≤ 10 ms for 10 k jobs + 1 k accounts fairshare tree traversal
Scheduling-pass CPU budget ≤ 1 physical core no parallelism inside a pass (P8 determinism)
Storage I/O (WAL) Sequential writes only; O_DSYNC or fdatasync per record no random writes in hot path
File descriptors (controld) ≤ 16 384 (raise RLIMIT_NOFILE) 4 k noded conns + 4 k CLI conns + WAL + misc
File descriptors (noded) ≤ 1 024 one fd per running step (stdin/stdout/stderr) + cgroup fds
TCP keepalive (noded→controld) 60 s idle, 10 s interval, 3 probes detect silent node loss faster than 3× heartbeat
Availability target 99.9% scheduler availability (≤ 8.7 h/yr downtime) single controller; 99.95% with HA (Phase 12)
Durability target 0 committed jobs lost across controller restart WAL fsync before ack; I5
Double-allocation tolerance 0 occurrences (hard invariant I1) enforced by bitmap transaction + WAL ordering
Scheduling correctness (conformance) 100% pass on conformance fixture suite (Phase 14) fixtures encode Slurm semantics

4.2 Deployment Assumptions

Assumption Value
OS Linux 5.10+ (cgroup v2 required for noded); controller: Linux 5.10+
CPU architecture x86-64 primary; aarch64 secondary (no platform-specific intrinsics in hot path)
Network 1–100 Gbit/s Ethernet or InfiniBand; control-plane uses Ethernet management network
Shared storage (HA) POSIX filesystem with fsync semantics (NFS, GPFS, or local NVMe replication)
Clock sync NTP/PTP; clocks within 1 s; timestamps used for age factor and backfill time estimation only
Node homogeneity Partitions may mix CPU-only and GPU nodes; heterogeneous memory sizes supported
Rust toolchain stable Rust ≥ 1.78; Tokio 1.x; no nightly features

4.3 Failure Budget

Failure type Budget Recovery mechanism
Controller crash (no HA) RTO ≤ 30 s; RPO = 0 committed jobs WAL replay; Phase 3
Controller crash (HA mode) RTO ≤ 60 s; RPO = 0 standby promotion; Phase 12
Compute node loss ≤ 90 s detection + ≤ 5 s requeue heartbeat timeout → requeue; Phase 10
Partial network partition (noded unreachable) Treat as node Down after 3 missed heartbeats conservative: never assume node is running if unreachable
WAL write failure Refuse submit; return error to user; do not advance state no silent data loss
Double-allocation attempt Panic in debug; return AllocError::Conflict in release; never commit I1 is a hard invariant

5. Required Reading

Books

# Title / Author Chapters to Study Concepts to Extract Applied In
B1 Feitelson & Rudolph — Job Scheduling Strategies for Parallel Processing (Springer LNCS 949, 1995–2003) All editions; chapters on backfill, utilization metrics, workload characterization, runtime-estimate fidelity EASY backfill genesis; why runtime-estimate honesty is a correctness property; utilization vs. bounded-wait tradeoff; space-sharing models §6 P4; Phase 7 (EASY backfill algorithm, I4, backfill depth limit); Phase 14 (workload conformance fixtures)
B2 Buyya (ed.) — High Performance Cluster Computing (Prentice Hall, 1999) Vol. 1 ch. 10–14: resource management models, space-sharing vs. time-sharing, node-level isolation Partitioning semantics, QoS definitions, allocation granularity that fed early cluster schedulers; partition limit enforcement shapes Phase 5 §6 P1; Phase 5 (partition limits); Phase 9 (QoS tiers); §8 ResourceSpec design
B3 Foster & Kesselman — The Grid: Blueprint for a New Computing Infrastructure (2nd ed., 2004) Ch. 6 (resource management), Ch. 7 (scheduling), Ch. 11 (accounting) GRAM / DRMAA contracts; JSDL resource-spec ancestry; usage-record data model → TRES lineage; multi-site resource brokering contrast §8 ResourceSpec (JSDL lineage, S1); Phase 11 (TRES accounting, OGF UR lineage, S2)
B4 Tanenbaum & Woodhull — Operating Systems: Design and Implementation (3rd ed.) Ch. 2 (process management), Ch. 3 (scheduling), Ch. 5 (I/O and resource accounting) Bitmap-based resource tracking; cgroup-style process isolation concept; multilevel scheduler fairness theory applicable to Phase 8 Phase 4 (CoreBitmap design, ADR-11); Phase 6 (cgroup hierarchy, noded launcher); §7 I2
B5 McKenney — Is Parallel Programming Hard? (kernel.org PDF, latest ed.) Chapters on locking, RCU, memory ordering, lock-free data structures Lock-free bitmap operations; memory-barrier requirements around CoreBitmap compare-and-swap in hpcsched-alloc; ABA problem mitigation Phase 4 (CoreBitmap CAS correctness, ADR-11); §7 I1 enforcement in hpcsched-alloc
B6 Silberschatz, Galvin, Gagne — Operating System Concepts (10th ed.) Ch. 5 (CPU scheduling — multilevel feedback queues), Ch. 8 (deadlock), Ch. 10 (virtual memory) Priority-queue theory; deadlock avoidance when allocating multiple TRES simultaneously; scheduler priority inversion patterns Phase 5 (PendingQueue ordering); Phase 8 (multifactor priority); Phase 4 (deadlock avoidance on multi-node alloc)
B7 Kleppmann — Designing Data-Intensive Applications (O'Reilly, 2017) Ch. 7 (transactions), Ch. 9 (consistency/consensus), Ch. 11 (stream / WAL), Ch. 5 (replication) WAL write-ahead semantics for hpcsched-state; checkpoint + replay correctness; leader election for Phase 12 HA; fencing with distributed locks Phase 3 (WAL, P6, ADR-06); Phase 12 (HA failover, fencing, I9); §7 I5 replay invariant
B8 Burns, Beda, Hightower — Kubernetes: Up & Running (3rd ed.) Scheduler design appendix; resource-request/limit model; preemption priority classes Contrasting approach: declarative desired-state vs. imperative allocation; lessons on preemption API surface; QoS class mapping to hpcsched QoS tiers §6 P2 (why central allocator vs. Kubernetes desired-state); Phase 9 (QoS preemption tiers); Comparative Architecture Table
B9 Gray & Reuter — Transaction Processing: Concepts and Techniques (Morgan Kaufmann, 1992) Ch. 9 (logging and recovery), Ch. 10 (checkpoint), Ch. 4 (ACID) ARIES-style WAL flush rules; LSN-based checkpoint; idempotent log replay — directly maps to hpcsched-state WAL design; force/steal buffer policy Phase 3 (WAL format, CRC framing, ADR-05); Phase 12 (checkpoint format, LSN tracking); §7 I5
B10 Herlihy & Shavit — The Art of Multiprocessor Programming (2nd ed.) Ch. 7 (spin locks), Ch. 9 (linked lists), Ch. 13 (concurrent queues) Correct concurrent priority-queue for PendingQueue; ABA-safe bitmap CAS; reader-writer lock for node inventory reads during scheduling pass Phase 4 (CoreBitmap CAS, ADR-12); Phase 5 (PendingQueue RwLock pattern, ADR-13); §7 I1
B11 Bovet & Cesati — Understanding the Linux Kernel (3rd ed.) Ch. 7 (process scheduling), Ch. 17 (cgroups), Ch. 3 (process descriptor) Linux scheduler internals behind hpcsched-noded cgroup enforcement; cpu.shares, memory.limit_in_bytes kernel interface Phase 6 (cgroup v2 hierarchy, ADR-29, S4); noded cgroup.rs; §7 I2 (TRES capacity enforcement)
