Operational Readiness

Metrics Strategy

Expose all metrics via Prometheus /metrics endpoint on controld (port 9100) and each noded (port 9101). Use prometheus crate with pre-registered descriptors. Label cardinality budget: never label by job_id or user (unbounded); use {partition, qos} for job-aggregate metrics. Histograms use exponential buckets scaled to expected SLO range.

controld metrics:

Metric Type Labels Description
scheduler_pending_jobs Gauge partition Pending queue depth per partition
scheduler_running_jobs Gauge partition, qos Running job count
scheduler_completing_jobs Gauge partition Jobs in Completing state (epilog)
scheduling_pass_duration_seconds Histogram pass_type (main/backfill) Latency per scheduling pass; buckets 0.01–30s
scheduling_pass_jobs_evaluated_total Counter pass_type Candidate jobs evaluated per pass
scheduling_pass_jobs_allocated_total Counter pass_type Jobs successfully allocated per pass
backfill_jobs_started_total Counter partition Jobs started via backfill (Phase 7)
backfill_reservation_lookahead_seconds Gauge — Top job reservation window width
cluster_utilization_ratio Gauge resource (cpu/mem/gpu) Aggregate TRES utilization
rpc_requests_total Counter method, status RPC call counts
rpc_duration_seconds Histogram method RPC latency; buckets 1ms–10s
wal_write_duration_seconds Histogram — WAL fsync latency; buckets 1ms–5s
wal_lsn_current Gauge — Current WAL LSN (for standby lag tracking)
wal_records_total Counter record_type WAL record throughput
heartbeat_timeout_total Counter — Missed heartbeat events
fairshare_gini_coefficient Gauge — Inequality of fairshare distribution; 0=equal, 1=max
preemption_events_total Counter mode (suspend/requeue/checkpoint) Preemptions issued
job_queue_time_seconds Histogram partition, qos Time from submit to Running
job_run_duration_seconds Histogram partition, qos Actual run time per completed job
acct_tres_seconds_total Counter account, tres Accounting throughput (aggregate; no per-user label)
invariant_check_failures_total Counter invariant (I1..I10) Invariant violation events (should always be 0)

noded metrics (per node):

Metric Type Labels Description
node_utilization_ratio Gauge resource (cpu/mem/gpu) Fraction of TRES in use on this node
node_step_processes_total Gauge — Currently running job step processes
node_cgroup_oom_events_total Counter — OOM kills from cgroup enforcement
node_heartbeat_send_duration_seconds Histogram — Heartbeat RPC round-trip

Tracing Strategy

Use tracing crate with tracing-opentelemetry exporter (OTLP → Jaeger or Tempo). Instrument:

Instrumentation point Span granularity Key span attributes
Scheduling pass (main) One root span per pass pass_type=main, jobs_evaluated, jobs_allocated, duration_ms
Scheduling pass (backfill) One root span; child per backfill candidate reservation_start, jobs_fitted, top_job_id
RPC handler Per-request span method, user, job_id, status
WAL write Per-record span record_type, lsn, fsync_ms
Job lifecycle event Span event on root job span from_state, to_state, node_id, reason
Allocation attempt Child of scheduling span job_id, candidate_nodes, result
Heartbeat processing Per-node span (sampled) node_id, delta_cores, delta_mem

Sampling: 100% for RPC errors; 1% for successful RPCs; 100% for scheduling-pass latency > p99 SLO threshold; head-based sampling with tail-override for error traces. Trace context propagated from CLI via gRPC metadata (traceparent header, W3C TraceContext).

Structured Logging

JSON-formatted via tracing-subscriber with json layer. Every log record includes: ts, level, target (crate::module), job_id (when applicable), node_id (when applicable), span_id.

Level Usage
ERROR Invariant violations, WAL corruption, fencing failures
WARN Spec validation rejection, heartbeat anomaly, capacity mismatch
INFO Job state transitions, scheduling pass start/end, node state changes
DEBUG Allocation attempt detail, priority computation inputs
TRACE Bitmap operations, individual RPC decode/encode

Avoid per-job-per-core logging at INFO; use DEBUG/TRACE for bitmap-level events. Key structured log events:

Event Level Required fields
Job submitted INFO job_id, user, partition, spec summary
Job state transition INFO job_id, from, to, reason, node_list
Scheduling pass summary INFO pass_type, evaluated, allocated, duration_ms
Allocation attempt fail DEBUG job_id, reason, candidate_nodes_tried
Backfill plan computed DEBUG top_job_id, reservation_time, fitted_jobs
Node state change INFO node_id, from, to, reason
WAL record written DEBUG record_type, lsn, job_id
Invariant check fail ERROR invariant, details, affected_job_id, affected_node_id
Preemption issued WARN preempted_job_id, preemptor_job_id, mode, grace_ms
Fencing event WARN event, controller_id, lease_expiry

Dashboard Design

Four dashboard tiers, all sourced from Prometheus + Jaeger/Tempo:

D1: Cluster Overview — pending/running/completed job counts over time; cluster TRES utilization gauges (cpu/mem/gpu) with high-watermark lines; scheduling pass latency heatmap (p50/p95/p99); node state distribution pie (idle/allocated/mixed/down/drain); job throughput (starts/completions per minute).

D2: Scheduling Health — backfill efficiency ratio (backfill_jobs/main_pass_jobs); queue age heatmap (p50/p95/p99 wait time per partition); fair-share Gini coefficient trending; preemption events rate; top-N accounts by TRES-hours consumed; backfill depth histogram.

D3: Infrastructure — controld WAL fsync latency p99; noded heartbeat miss rate per node; RPC error rate by method; controller memory RSS and heap allocation; fencing events timeline; standby lag (WAL LSN delta).

D4: Accounting / Fairness — TRES-hours by account/user (bar chart, top 20); association tree utilization; QoS tier distribution of running jobs; per-partition utilization over 7 days; job success/failure rates by partition.

Alerting Philosophy

Alert on symptoms and SLO budget burn, not on individual metrics crossing arbitrary thresholds.

Alert Condition Severity Action
SchedulingLoopSlowBurnRate Pass latency p99 > 2s for 5m WARNING Reduce backfill depth; profile
SchedulingLoopCritical Pass latency p99 > 10s for 2m CRITICAL Page on-call; pause backfill
NodeFlapping Node transitions Down>Up>Down ≥ 3 in 10m WARNING Drain node; investigate hardware
PendingQueueGrowth Pending depth growing >10%/min for 15m WARNING Investigate throughput; check allocator
WalFsyncSlow WAL fsync p99 > 200ms WARNING Check storage I/O; consider NVMe
ControllerFailover Standby promoted CRITICAL Verify fencing; check old primary; audit WAL
FairshareGiniHigh Gini > 0.7 for 1h WARNING Audit account usage; check decay config
InvariantViolation Any I1/I2/I9 check failure logged CRITICAL Emergency: halt scheduling; page

SLO Definitions

SLO Target Measurement Window Burn-rate Alert Threshold
Job submission ack latency p99 < 200ms 7-day rolling 5× budget burn for 1h
Scheduling pass latency p99 (main) < 1s for ≤10k pending jobs 7-day rolling 5× for 5m
Scheduling pass latency p99 (backfill) < 2s 7-day rolling 5× for 5m
Node registration on first heartbeat < 5s 7-day rolling —
Controller restart + WAL replay < 60s for ≤1M WAL records measured at restart —
HA failover completion < 30s per-event Page if exceeded
Job state consistency after failover 100% (zero double-allocation) per-event Page on violation

Incident Response

Step Action Tool / Command
1. Detect Alert fires or user reports PagerDuty; squeue/sinfo analog from login node
2. Acknowledge Within 5 min; set incident channel PagerDuty
3. Triage Check D1 dashboard; note pass latency, queue depth, node states Grafana; sinfo -a
4. Contain If loop unstable: reduce backfill depth via admin RPC; if node issue: drain sadmin backfill-depth 50; scontrol update NodeName=X State=drain
5. Investigate Pull scheduling-pass trace; inspect WAL tail; grep invariant violation logs Jaeger; hpcsched-state --tail 100; journalctl
6. Recover Execute relevant runbook RB-01 through RB-08 below
7. Verify Confirm SLOs recovering; queue draining; no invariant logs Grafana; structured log grep
8. Post-mortem Within 48h; update fuzz corpus + invariant tests GitHub issue; cargo-fuzz seed

Runbooks

Runbook Trigger Key Steps
RB-01: Controller WAL Recovery WAL checksum error at startup Identify last clean record; truncate; restore from checkpoint; replay
RB-02: HA Failover Manual Trigger Primary unhealthy; standby not auto-promoting SIGKILL primary; verify lease expired; promote standby via admin command
RB-03: Node Down Recovery Node in Down state after hardware fix Drain remaining jobs; restart noded; verify heartbeat received; set node to Idle via scontrol analog
RB-04: Scheduling Loop Hung Pass latency SLO breached Reduce backfill_max_jobs_per_pass; inspect flamegraph; if deadlock — restart controld (standby takes over)
RB-05: Double-Allocation Recovery I1/I9 invariant violation logged Halt scheduling (admin flag); drain affected nodes; reconcile WAL; verify bitmap consistency; re-enable
RB-06: Fair-Share Reset Usage data corrupted or Gini runaway Export current usage; reset association usage table; re-import from sacct; restart fairshare decay timer
RB-07: Queue Depth Runaway Pending queue growing without bound Identify top submitting user/account; apply temporary rate limit; investigate job failures causing requeue storms
RB-08: Clock Skew Recovery noded/controld clock delta > threshold Restart NTP on affected node; verify sync; if skew > 60s drain node; use controld authoritative time

Backup and Restore

Artifact Backup method Frequency Retention RPO RTO
WAL stream Continuous append to durable store (NFS/S3/shared block) Real-time (fsync per record) 7 days rolling ≤ fsync interval (500ms default) WAL replay time
Checkpoint snapshot Periodic write to durable store Every 5 min + clean shutdown Last 10 checkpoints 5 min < 60s for ≤1M WAL records
sacct SQLite DB Daily dump via .backup pragma Daily 90 days 24h (accounting data only) Minutes
Config files Version-controlled in git On change Full history Last commit Manual apply

Restore procedure:

  1. Stop controld on all nodes.
  2. Copy the target checkpoint file to the local WAL path.
  3. Truncate WAL to the checkpoint's end_lsn.
  4. Start controld with --replay-from-checkpoint; verify all records pass CRC32C.
  5. Verify JobState counts match pre-failure sacct data.
  6. Promote standby or start primary normally.

Standby always has a live WAL tail: the RPO for state data is ≤ last fsync interval; accounting data has up to 24h RPO from the sacct backup cadence.

Deployment Topology

                [NFS/shared durable store]
                  WAL + checkpoints
                       ^  ^
                       |  |
           controld-primary   controld-standby
           (hpcsched-controld) (hpcsched-controld)
                       |
         +----------+----------+
         |          |          |
       noded-0    noded-1   noded-N
       (compute)  (compute)  (compute)
  • Primary + one (or more) standbys; standbys are read-only replica subscribers.
  • noded runs on every compute node; started by init system (systemd).
  • CLI binaries run from login nodes; they connect to controld via TCP/TLS.

Canary and Rolling Upgrade

  • Canary: deploy new controld to standby first; promote to primary for one partition only; monitor SLOs for 30 min; if clean, promote for all partitions.
  • Rolling noded upgrade: drain one node at a time; upgrade noded binary; verify heartbeat; undrain; proceed.
  • Zero-downtime controld upgrade: upgrade standby; failover to it; upgrade old primary; failover back (optional).
  • Rollback: revert binary on standby; failover to previous version; WAL format backward-compatible within a major version (see Compatibility & Versioning).

Migration Strategy

  • Config migrations (partitions, accounts, QoS tiers): apply via admin RPC; validated before commit; take effect at next scheduling pass; no restart required.
  • Schema migrations (WAL record additions): additive only within a MAJOR version; old controld skips unknown record types.
  • Node additions: noded on new node starts; registers via first heartbeat; controld admits to inventory after cert validation; schedulable within one heartbeat interval.
  • Node removals: drain node (I6); wait for running jobs to complete or requeue; deregister via admin command; revoke cert.
  • Account/association tree changes: applied transactionally; fairshare recomputed on next pass; historical usage preserved.
  • sacct DB schema migrations: forward-only via embedded migration table; run at startup; rollback by restoring backup.
  • WAL format MAJOR migration: run hpcsched-migrate vN vN+1 offline; verify checksum of output; swap in; restart cluster.

Compatibility & Versioning

Semantic Versioning Policy

MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH per semver.org. controld, noded, and CLI binaries carry the same MAJOR version.

Version bump Trigger
PATCH Bug fix; no protocol or schema change
MINOR Additive proto field; additive WAL record type; new CLI flag; backward-compatible
MAJOR Breaking proto change; WAL format incompatible; type rename; API removal

Wire Protocol Compatibility (hpcsched-proto)

Encoding: protobuf3 via prost. Frame format: 4-byte big-endian length prefix + serialized proto bytes. TLS 1.3 mandatory for all connections.

  • Proto3 unknown-field semantics: new fields added in a MINOR release are silently ignored by older readers — no deserialization error.
  • Field numbers are immutable once assigned; deprecated fields get reserved declarations; no field number reuse within a MAJOR.
  • Message versioning: each top-level RPC message carries a uint32 protocol_version field; controld rejects connections whose protocol_version < min_supported; min_supported increments only at MAJOR.
  • A noded at version N.M is wire-compatible with a controld at version N.M or N.M+1.
  • Breaking changes (field rename, removal, semantic change) require MAJOR bump and explicit migration path documented in CHANGELOG.
  • Rolling upgrade guarantee: controld at vN.M+1 speaks vN.M wire protocol; enables rolling noded upgrades without cluster downtime.
Compatibility axis Guarantee Constraint
controld vN.M ↔ noded vN.M Full —
controld vN.M+1 ↔ noded vN.M Backward-compatible additive fields ignored by noded
controld vN.M ↔ noded vN.M+1 Forward-compatible no new required fields used
controld vN+1 ↔ noded vN Not guaranteed MAJOR = coordinated upgrade required

WAL / Checkpoint Format Compatibility

WAL record wire layout:

[4B length][4B CRC32C][1B record_type][2B protocol_version][N bytes protobuf payload]
  • Length and CRC32C are outside the proto payload; CRC covers the type+version+payload bytes.
  • New record types added in MINOR: unknown record_type values are skipped by old controld at replay (length-delimited skip).
  • Field additions within existing record types: proto3 unknown-field safety; no deserialization error.
  • WAL LSN: 64-bit monotonically increasing sequence number assigned at append; used for checkpoint anchoring and standby lag tracking.
  • Checkpoint format: CBOR-encoded snapshot of full controller state (all jobs, nodes, allocations, fairshare tree); header contains schema_version: u32, lsn: u64, sha256: [u8; 32].
  • MINOR checkpoint migrations: new fields in checkpoint have documented default values; old controld deserializes and ignores unknown fields (serde deny_unknown_fields = false).
  • MAJOR checkpoint migrations: provide offline migration tool (hpcsched-migrate checkpoint) that reads vN CBOR, transforms to vN+1 schema, writes new CBOR with updated schema_version; operator must run before starting vN+1 controld.
  • WAL MAJOR migrations: hpcsched-migrate wal reads vN frames, re-encodes as vN+1 frames; CRC recomputed; old WAL archived, not deleted.

Core Type Evolution (hpcsched-core)

Type Evolution rule Forbidden in MINOR
JobState enum New variants in MINOR only; discriminants stable Rename, remove, or reorder existing variants
NodeState enum Same rule as JobState Same
ResourceSpec New optional fields with Option<T> + defaults; serialized with proto3 Remove, rename, or change type of existing fields
CoreBitmap / GpuSet Width can grow (zero-extend on deserialization); stored as fixed-width bytes with length prefix Shrink width; change bit ordering
PriorityFactors New weight fields default to 0 in MINOR (no effect on existing ranking); old weights stable Change existing weight semantics without MAJOR bump
Allocation Additive fields; nodes: Vec<(NodeId, CoreBitmap, GpuSet, mem)> element type stable Reorder tuple elements; change NodeId semantics
StateLog record variants Additive in MINOR; each variant is a separate proto message Change existing variant field numbers
HeartbeatMsg / LaunchMsg Additive fields in MINOR Change mandatory fields to optional without MAJOR

API Stability (CLI + RPC)

  • RPC method signatures (service name + method name + request/response types) stable within MAJOR; new optional request fields in MINOR.
  • RPC error codes: numeric codes stable within MAJOR; error message strings are not stable.
  • CLI flag names stable within MAJOR; new flags added in MINOR; existing flags never change semantics within MAJOR.
  • Removed CLI flags: deprecated in MINOR N.M; removed no earlier than N.M+2.
  • hpcsched-cli human-readable text output is not a stable API (format may change in MINOR).
  • hpcsched-cli JSON output (--json / --format=json) is stable within MAJOR; field names and types follow the corresponding hpcsched-core type names.
  • Environment variable names (e.g., HPCSCHED_CONTROLLER_ADDR, HPCSCHED_LOG_LEVEL) stable within MAJOR.
  • Admin RPC surface (partition create/update, association management, node state control) stable within MAJOR; admin-only methods are labeled in proto with option (admin_only) = true.

Deprecation Policy

  1. Mark deprecated in MINOR N.M: #[deprecated(since = "N.M", note = "...")] in Rust; [deprecated=true] option in proto; CHANGELOG entry with migration guidance.
  2. Emit structured WARN log (level WARN, field deprecated_feature) on first use per process lifetime.
  3. Remove no earlier than N.M+2 (two MINOR versions later) or next MAJOR — whichever comes first in the release calendar.
  4. Proto fields: add reserved <field_number>; reserved "<field_name>"; declaration in same commit as removal; never reuse the number.
  5. WAL record types: record_type values are never reused; deprecated record types are kept as skippable no-ops in the replay loop indefinitely.
  6. Config keys: deprecated keys emit WARN at startup; removed in MAJOR only.

Rolling-Upgrade Invariants

  • At no point during a rolling upgrade is a resource double-allocated (I1, I9 hold continuously).
  • Fencing lease mechanism is protocol-version-agnostic (based on lease timestamp, not message format); holds across controld version mismatch.
  • A mixed-version cluster (controld at vN.M+1, noded at vN.M) satisfies I1–I10 for the duration of the upgrade window.
  • WAL written by vN.M+1 is replayable by vN.M controld as long as no new-MINOR record types were emitted; if they were, the standby must be upgraded to vN.M+1 before failover.
  • Upgrade ordering: always upgrade controld (standby first, then primary via failover) before upgrading noded instances; never upgrade noded to a version ahead of controld.
  • Downgrade path within a MINOR: revert binary; WAL records written by newer version with additive fields are harmless to older replayer; if new record types were written, truncate WAL to the last pre-upgrade LSN (documented in release notes when applicable).

Schema / Storage Format Version Table

Artifact Format Version field Compatibility scope Migration tool
WAL records protobuf3 frames + CRC32C record_type discriminant + protocol_version field Unknown types skipped in same MAJOR hpcsched-migrate wal vN vN+1
Checkpoint CBOR schema_version: u32 in header Monotone; additive fields in MINOR hpcsched-migrate checkpoint vN vN+1
noded-local step state JSON spool files version key Read by noded only; discarded on upgrade None (ephemeral)
sacct DB SQLite + embedded migrations schema_version table (migration registry) Forward-only; rollback via backup restore Embedded in hpcsched-acct startup
RPC protocol protobuf3 uint32 protocol_version in each message Unknown fields ignored; MAJOR = wire break Protocol negotiation at handshake
Config files TOML config_version: u32 field Parsed by controld; unknown fields rejected (strict) Manual; documented in CHANGELOG

Compatibility Testing Requirements

  • CI: cross-version wire tests: controld vN+1 ↔ noded vN for all MINOR bumps.
  • CI: WAL replay test: WAL written by vN replays correctly under vN+1.
  • CI: checkpoint migration test: hpcsched-migrate round-trips state without loss.
  • Fuzz (Phase 14): proto deserialization fuzz for all RPC message types.
  • Conformance fixtures (Phase 14): golden-file tests encoding documented Slurm semantics; must pass across versions without change.

Testing & Verification Strategy

Unit Testing

Target Harness What is Verified
CoreBitmap / GpuSet ops #[test] in hpcsched-alloc alloc/free idempotency; double-free panic; OR/AND/count correctness
JobState FSM #[test] in hpcsched-core every legal transition; every illegal transition returns Err; no skip over Completing
PriorityFactors computation #[test] in hpcsched-fairshare formula output for known inputs; weight=0 annuls factor; monotonicity
WAL encode/decode round-trip #[test] in hpcsched-state every StateLog variant serializes and deserializes to identical value
Allocator constraint matching parameterized #[test] feature intersection, contiguous-node packing, NUMA topology hint
BackfillPlan validity #[test] in hpcsched-sched backfilled job's end ≤ reservation start (I4); no resource overlap with reserved set (I1)
Association tree decay #[test] in hpcsched-fairshare decay formula; shares sum; child promoted when parent depleted
Limit enforcement #[test] in hpcsched-acct GrpTRES, MaxJobs, MaxWall; submit rejects over-limit; running job not killed retroactively

Integration Testing

  • Controller + two in-process nodeds: submit → queue → allocate → launch → complete lifecycle; assert JobState::Completed and TRES accounting match.
  • WAL replay: run 50 jobs, crash controller mid-flight (inject std::process::abort via hook), replay from WAL+checkpoint, assert state equals pre-crash snapshot (I5).
  • Node down / requeue: mark noded silent; wait heartbeat timeout; assert running jobs transition to Requeued (I6, I7).
  • Failover HA: primary writes checkpoint, promote standby; submit new job on standby, assert no double-allocation (I9).
  • Priority ordering: submit 10 jobs with varying fair-share and age; assert scheduling pass selects strictly in priority order (P8).
  • Partition limits: submit beyond max_nodes; assert excess stays Pending; as slots free, jobs drain in order.
  • EASY backfill: one large top job reserves future window; verify smaller low-priority jobs fill gap without delaying top (P4, I4).
  • QoS preemption: high-QoS job arrives while low-QoS jobs run; assert low-QoS jobs requeued within grace period; allocation reassigned.
  • CLI round-trip: sbatch → squeue → scancel; assert cancel transitions to Cancelled; sacct returns correct TRES-seconds (I10).

Property-Based Testing (proptest / quickcheck)

Property Generator
After any sequence of alloc/free ops, node capacity invariant holds (I2) random job spec streams
Fair-share priority is total order with no ties among distinct jobs (I8) random association tree + usage
PendingQueue pop order equals sorted priority order random insert sequences
WAL record set is a function of operations applied (no phantom records) arbitrary op sequences
Allocator: alloc then free leaves bitmap identical to initial state random node configs
Backfill plan: every (job, slot) pair is resource-disjoint random reservation windows

Fuzzing

  • hpcsched-proto wire fuzzer (cargo fuzz / libFuzzer): feed arbitrary bytes into every message parser; assert no panic, no UB, all Err paths reached.
  • WAL replay fuzzer: truncated, bit-flipped, reordered WAL segments; assert controller either recovers clean state or returns structured error; never hangs.
  • CLI argument fuzzer: AFL-style corpus from valid squeue/sbatch args; detect parser panics.
  • Scheduler state fuzzer: inject random JobSubmitted / NodeDown / NodeUp events; assert invariants I1–I3 hold after each step.

Deterministic-Replay / Determinism Testing

  • Capture full event trace (RPC calls, timestamps, node reports) to deterministic replay log.
  • Re-run trace against fresh controller; assert identical scheduling decisions, identical allocation assignments, identical priority rankings.
  • Validates P8: scheduling is a pure function of state.
  • Replay is the primary regression harness for scheduling bugs: reproduce exact production traces from logs.
  • Seed random number generator (if any) via config; assert determinism across compiler versions.

Chaos / Fault Injection

Fault Injection Mechanism Pass Criterion
Node silent (heartbeat drop) iptables block / mock timeout jobs requeued within 2× heartbeat interval
Controller kill -9 mid-alloc SIGKILL via test harness standby resumes; no double-allocation
Disk full (WAL write failure) ulimit -f 0 in child controller returns error to client; does not corrupt state
Clock jump forward 1 hour injected clock source time-limit enforcement triggers correctly
Duplicate LaunchMsg replay same launch RPC noded is idempotent; job step not spawned twice
Partial checkpoint (truncated) write half of snapshot replay falls back to previous checkpoint + WAL tail

Soak Testing

  • 24-hour soak: 500 simulated nodes, constant inflow of 100 jobs/min (mix of short/long, small/large), random node failures (5% churn/hr).
  • Assertions polled every 60 s: I1 (no double-alloc), I2 (capacity not exceeded), queue depth stable, no memory growth >5% per hour.
  • Tracks scheduling-pass latency percentiles; alert if p99 exceeds Phase 14 target (see Performance Engineering).

Conformance Validation

  • Fixture suite encoding canonical Slurm semantics:
    • Backfill: specific job mix → specific backfill plan (matches reference behavior).
    • Fair-share decay: known usage table → known priority ranking after N periods.
    • Preemption ordering: QoS tiers → correct victims chosen in correct order.
    • State transitions: each FSM arc tested with pre/post state assertions.
  • Fixtures are YAML; runner applies them via a headless controller stub.
  • Fixtures are the regression gate: no new code merges if any fixture regresses.

Performance Regression

  • Benchmark suite (see Performance Engineering) runs in CI on every merge to main.
  • Baseline stored as JSON in benches/baselines/; CI fails if any benchmark regresses > 5%.
  • Tracked metrics: scheduling-pass wall time, allocator throughput (allocs/s), WAL sync latency, fair-share recompute time.

Differential Testing (Reference Comparison)

  • No binary-compatible reference exists; instead: a minimal Python simulation (tools/sched_sim.py) encodes the same scheduling pass, priority formula, and backfill logic.
  • For a random workload trace, compare Python sim output vs. hpcsched scheduling decisions; flag divergences.
  • Validates that the Rust implementation matches the spec's intent, not just itself.

Build & Release Engineering

Toolchain and Reproducibility

Concern Mechanism
Pinned toolchain rust-toolchain.toml: channel = stable, explicit version
Reproducible binary RUSTFLAGS=-C metadata=<hash>; SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH set in CI
Dependency lock Cargo.lock committed; --locked enforced in CI and release builds
Compiler version gate CI fails if rustc --version != pinned version
no_std audit for hpcsched-core #![no_std] compile check in CI to keep model crate portable

SBOM and Dependency Auditing

  • cargo-sbom generates CycloneDX SBOM on every release tag; artifact uploaded to GitHub Releases.
  • cargo audit runs in CI; blocks merge on any RUSTSEC advisory affecting direct or transitive deps.
  • cargo deny enforces: license allowlist (MIT/Apache-2.0/BSD); no duplicated transitive crates (allowed exceptions listed in deny.toml).
  • Dependabot or Renovate bot opens PRs for patch bumps weekly.

Artifact Signing and Provenance

  • Release binaries (hpcsched-controld, hpcsched-noded, hpcsched-cli bundle) signed with cosign keyless (Sigstore) on GitHub Actions OIDC token.
  • SLSA level 2: provenance attestation (slsa-github-generator) attached to every release artifact.
  • Container image (if provided): signed with cosign; SBOM attached as image attestation.
  • Checksums (sha256) published alongside binaries; verified by install script before extraction.

CI/CD Topology

PR branch:
  lint (cargo fmt --check, cargo clippy --deny warnings)
  → unit tests (cargo test --workspace)
  → integration tests (cargo test -p hpcsched-integration)
  → fuzz (10 s smoke: cargo fuzz run <target> -- -max_total_time=10)
  → benchmark delta (compare vs. main baseline)

main merge:
  full test suite → benchmark baseline update → soak (short, 30 min) → artifact build

release tag vX.Y.Z:
  reproducible release build → SBOM → sign → SLSA provenance → publish to GitHub Releases
  → crate publish (hpcsched-core, hpcsched-proto — public API crates only)
  • Matrix: linux/amd64, linux/aarch64; cross-compiled via cross.
  • Nightly job: full 24 h soak + full fuzz corpus run; failures open GitHub issues automatically.

Version Stamping

  • build.rs in each crate: embeds GIT_SHA, BUILD_DATE, CARGO_PKG_VERSION into binary via env! macros.
  • hpcsched-cli --version outputs: hpcsched 0.1.0 (abc1234 2026-06-05).
  • WAL records include schema version byte; controller rejects replaying WAL from incompatible future version.

Release Channels

Channel Trigger Audience
nightly main merge developers / CI integration
rc manual tag vX.Y.Z-rc.N cluster admins testing
stable manual tag vX.Y.Z production deployments
  • Protocol compatibility: hpcsched-proto declares a protocol version in the handshake; noded ≥ controld version required; minor-version backward compat guaranteed within a major.

Performance Engineering

Benchmarking Strategy

Benchmark Crate Harness Target
Scheduling pass, 10k pending jobs hpcsched-sched criterion < 100 ms p99
Scheduling pass, 1k pending jobs hpcsched-sched criterion < 10 ms p99
Allocator: alloc 256-node job, 1024-node cluster hpcsched-alloc criterion < 1 ms
WAL sync (fsync) latency hpcsched-state criterion < 5 ms p99 (NVMe)
Fair-share recompute, 10k associations hpcsched-fairshare criterion < 20 ms
Heartbeat message processing, 1k nodes/s hpcsched-controld criterion < 2 ms per tick
Priority queue insert/pop, 50k jobs hpcsched-queue criterion amortized O(log n)
Backfill scan, 500 candidate jobs hpcsched-sched criterion < 30 ms
  • All criterion benchmarks use fixed seeds and synthetic workload generators for reproducibility (P8).
  • Latency measured as wall time on isolated c5.xlarge (4 vCPU, 8 GB) in CI; results attached to PR.

Quantified Performance Targets

Each target below is derived from §4.1 (Quantified Engineering Targets) and gated in CI at the phase named; a PR that regresses a gated metric beyond its threshold fails the pipeline (ADR-23).

# Metric Target Phase Gate
M1 Scheduling-pass latency, 10k pending / 4k nodes p50 ≤ 200 ms, p99 ≤ 800 ms Phase 5 (criterion regression); re-verified at scale in Phase 14
M2 Backfill-pass latency, 500-candidate depth limit p99 ≤ 500 ms Phase 7 (benches/backfill_depth.rs)
M3 Submit RPC latency, WAL fsync included p99 ≤ 50 ms Phase 3 (WAL sync criterion bench)
M4 Heartbeat processing time per event ≤ 10 ms/event at 4,096 nodes/interval Phase 2 (heartbeat criterion bench)
M5 Allocation transaction time (bitmap AND + popcount) ≤ 1 µs per node evaluated Phase 4 (benches allocator suite)
M6 Fair-share / priority recompute, 10k jobs + 1k accounts ≤ 10 ms Phase 8 (fairshare criterion bench)
M7 Controller restart + WAL replay, 100k records ≤ 30 s Phase 3 gate; soak-scale re-check in Phase 14
M8 HA failover RTO ≤ 60 s Phase 12 (kill-primary integration test)
M9 Controller RSS, 10k pending + 4k nodes ≤ 2 GB Phase 14 (tests/scale, massif-checked)
M10 noded RSS per node ≤ 64 MB Phase 6 (noded memory profiling)
M11 Priority queue insert/pop, 50k jobs amortized O(log n); criterion regression ≤ 5% vs. baseline Phase 5 (benches queue suite)
M12 CLI squeue RPC round-trip, 10k jobs server-filtered response ≤ 5 MB; ≤ 200 ms end-to-end Phase 13 (CLI integration bench)

Profiling Methodology

  • CPU flamegraphs: cargo flamegraph --bin hpcsched-controld -- --replay <trace> via perf record + flamegraph.pl; focus on scheduling loop hot path.
  • Allocation profiling: heaptrack or dhat (via dhat crate feature); identify per-pass allocations in BackfillPlan construction and PendingQueue snapshot.
  • Lock contention: mutrace or perf lock; primary concern: the single global scheduling lock in controld.
  • Syscall overhead: strace -c on WAL writer; target < 1 fsync per job submission batch (coalesce writes).

Flamegraph Workflow

  1. Build with --release + debug symbols ([profile.release] debug = 1).
  2. Run replay trace via --replay logs/soak-trace.bin to produce deterministic CPU load.
  3. perf record -g --call-graph dwarf -F 999 -- hpcsched-controld --replay ...
  4. perf script | stackcollapse-perf.pl | flamegraph.pl > flame.svg
  5. Key hot paths to check: schedule_pass, alloc_nodes, compute_priority, wal_append.

Tracing Methodology

  • tracing crate with tracing-subscriber; spans on: schedule_pass (duration, jobs_considered, jobs_started), alloc_nodes (nodes_tried, nodes_selected), wal_append (bytes, sync_latency_ms).
  • Production: RUST_LOG=hpcsched=info; debug traces at debug level gated behind compile feature.
  • OTLP export (optional): spans to Jaeger/Tempo for distributed trace across controld + noded.
  • Per-pass structured log line: {pass_ms, pending, started, backfilled, utilization_pct} — primary operational metric.

Allocator Analysis

  • dhat heap profiler identifies short-lived Vec<NodeId> allocations in alloc_nodes; target: replace with stack-allocated SmallVec<[NodeId; 8]> for jobs ≤ 8 nodes.
  • CoreBitmap and GpuSet are fixed-size stack arrays — zero heap; assert no Box in hot alloc path via #[cfg(test)] assert_no_alloc macro on allocate().
  • PendingQueue snapshot for backfill: pre-allocated ring buffer; avoid per-pass Vec::clone.

Lock Analysis

  • Single Mutex<ControllerState> in controld held for the duration of schedule_pass — correct but serializing.
  • Profiling target: confirm pass duration is CPU-bound (scheduling logic), not lock-wait-bound (no contention from RPC handlers during pass).
  • RPC handlers enqueue work items; scheduling loop drains them at pass start — lock is held only during drain + pass, not during WAL fsync (which happens after lock release).
  • If p99 pass latency exceeds target, profile to verify: lock wait < 5% of pass time.

Syscall Analysis

  • WAL writer: batch multiple JobSubmitted records within a 5 ms window; single fsync per batch. Confirm via strace -e trace=fsync,write.
  • LaunchMsg RPC: one TCP send per noded; no per-core syscall. Confirm with strace -e trace=sendmsg.
  • Heartbeat aggregation: 1000 nodes × 1 heartbeat/30 s = ~33 msgs/s; negligible syscall rate.

Memory Profiling

  • valgrind --tool=massif on 10k-job soak; target peak RSS < 512 MB for controld at 10k concurrent jobs.
  • Per-job memory: Job struct target ≤ 256 bytes; Allocation ≤ 512 bytes; PriorityFactors ≤ 64 bytes.
  • Monitor VmRSS via /proc/self/status sampled each scheduling pass; alert if growth exceeds 1 MB/pass.

Tail-Latency Analysis

  • Scheduling pass latency measured as histogram (criterion's sample_size=1000); report p50/p95/p99/p999.
  • Backfill depth limit (configurable, default 500 candidates) is the primary p99 control knob.
  • WAL fsync latency measured separately; p99 target 5 ms; alert if storage degrades (relevant for Phase 12 HA).

Scalability Analysis

Dominant Scaling Pressures

Pressure Origin Growth Function
Scheduling-pass CPU O(pending × nodes) try-allocate quadratic worst case; bounded by depth limit
Backfill scan O(candidates × reservation_checks) linear in depth limit; tunable
Fair-share recompute O(associations) per pass linear; amortizable to background task
WAL storage O(jobs_submitted) unbounded requires periodic compaction (checkpoint + WAL truncation)
Node heartbeat fan-in O(nodes/heartbeat_interval) linear; 10k nodes = 333 msgs/s at 30 s interval — manageable
Accounting TRES records O(completed_jobs) linear; cold-tier archival after TTL

Scaling Ceilings

Dimension Ceiling Bottleneck
Pending jobs ~50k practical schedule-pass CPU (single-threaded pass)
Cluster nodes ~10k nodes heartbeat fan-in + inventory lock in controld
Concurrent running jobs ~20k Allocation map lookup; bitmap scan
Associations (users/accounts) ~100k fair-share tree traversal (O(depth))
WAL size without compaction ~10 GB/day at 1k submits/min disk; checkpoint + truncation required
Scheduling-pass latency at 10k pending ~80–100 ms allocator try-allocate inner loop

Scheduling-Pass Optimization Strategies

  • Eligible-set pre-filter (hpcsched-queue): exclude jobs blocked on limits/dependencies before pass; reduces pass size.
  • Depth limit (P9): configurable sched_max_depth; default 500; cap backfill scan independently.
  • Node preselect index: build inverted feature index (feature → node set) at node registration; alloc_nodes intersects bitmaps rather than scanning all nodes.
  • Amortize fair-share: recompute every N passes or when usage changes > threshold; not every pass.
  • Parallel allocation attempts: if pass becomes bottleneck, split pending queue into partition-isolated groups and allocate per-partition in parallel (no cross-partition resource contention).

Horizontal / Vertical Scaling

  • Vertical (primary path): controld is single-process; vertical CPU/memory scaling is effective up to ~10k nodes / 50k pending.
  • Horizontal (future Phase 14+): federated scheduling — multiple controllers own disjoint node partitions; jobs submitted to a federation broker. Out of scope for Phase 14 but architecture must not preclude it: partition ownership is encoded in Partition, controller identity in NodeId prefix.
  • Standby HA (Phase 12): active/standby pair; not load-sharing, only failover; no scheduling throughput increase.

Storage Growth

Store Growth Rate Mitigation
WAL ~1 KB/job event × submit rate checkpoint every N events; truncate WAL to post-checkpoint tail
Checkpoint O(nodes + running_jobs + pending_jobs) single file; overwrite on each checkpoint
Accounting records ~512 B/completed job archive completed records to cold store after 90 days
Metrics TSDB O(metrics × nodes × time) external Prometheus with 15-day retention; not in controld

Coordination Overhead

  • Single controller: no distributed consensus cost; all scheduling decisions are local.
  • Node daemons: stateless executors; heartbeat is the only coordination (UDP or TCP keepalive).
  • Phase 12 HA: standby tails WAL; no quorum protocol; failover is a one-way promotion — no coordination during steady state.
  • If federation is introduced: inter-controller coordination cost is O(cross-partition jobs); initially zero.

Memory Scaling

  • ControllerState at 10k nodes + 50k pending jobs:
    • Nodes: 10k × ~512 B (Node + bitmaps) = ~5 MB
    • Pending jobs: 50k × ~256 B (Job header) = ~13 MB
    • Allocations: 20k running × ~512 B = ~10 MB
    • Priority heap: 50k × 64 B = ~3 MB
    • Total estimate: ~35–50 MB; well within 512 MB RSS target.
  • Accounting TRES records: held in memory for active jobs only; completed jobs flushed to disk immediately.

Latency vs. Throughput Trade-offs

Knob Effect on Latency Effect on Throughput
sched_interval (pass frequency) lower → better job start latency lower → higher CPU overhead
backfill_depth higher → more backfill work per pass higher → better utilization
WAL batch window lower → lower submit latency lower → more fsyncs
Heartbeat interval lower → faster node-down detection lower → higher network load
  • Defaults tuned for a 1000-node cluster; all knobs exposed via config for site-specific tuning.
  • Phase 14 soak validates that chosen defaults sustain 95% utilization at < 5 min mean queue wait under synthetic HPC workload distributions (Feitelson workload model). Terms used above (eligible set, depth limit, TRES, checkpoint, fencing) are defined precisely in the Glossary.

Mental Models & First Principles

These are the compressed insights that distinguish an expert designer from someone who has read the code. Each is numbered MM1…MM8, names the exact §/phase where the blueprint applies it, states what a violation produces, and names the failure class you write without the model.

MM1. A scheduler is a transaction log with a priority index. Every scheduling decision — allocate, free, requeue — is a durable state mutation that must be ordered relative to all other mutations. The pending queue is just a view over uncommitted resource reservations. Expert insight: if you cannot replay your scheduling log to reconstruct the exact queue state, your log is missing a record. Predicts: WAL-before-ack correctness (P6, Phase 3). Bug class without it: job appears in queue after WAL loss but controller has already dispatched it — double-dispatch on restart. Applied in: §6 P6, §7 I5, Phase 3.2.

MM2. Bitmap allocation is a compare-and-set over a vector, not a bag of integers. A node's available cores are not a count; they are a set of specific logical CPU indices. Two jobs wanting "4 cores" may want disjoint sets. The correctness invariant is bitwise: (existing_alloc & new_alloc) == 0. Counting alone misses this. Expert insight: the only safe atomicity primitive is a read-modify-write over the entire bitmap under a lock. Predicts: I1 (no double-allocation). Bug class without it: two allocations for adjacent core ranges silently overlap at word boundaries. Applied in: §6 P3, §7 I1/I2, Phase 4.2.

MM3. Backfill correctness is a monotonicity property, not a greedy heuristic. The EASY invariant (Lifka 1995, P2) is: the reservation start for the top job must be non-decreasing after each backfill decision. Any backfilled job that could push that start rightward must be rejected — even if it fits in free nodes today. Predicts: I4. Bug class without it: backfill launches a job with a 3-hour limit into nodes reserved in 2 hours; top job starves. Applied in: §6 P4, §7 I4, Phase 7.2.

MM4. Fair-share without decay is a priority debt that never expires. Without usage decay, a group that burst-submitted 1000 jobs last month has permanently lower priority than all new groups. The half-life formula (P15) ensures the penalty is proportional to recency. Expert insight: select the decay half-life as the ethical "statute of limitations" for overconsumption, not a performance knob. Predicts: §6 P5 (hierarchical decay). Bug class without it: new research groups on the cluster can never compete with legacy power users; cluster becomes a political problem, not a technical one. Applied in: §8 P5, §8 Multifactor Priority Model, Phase 8.2.

MM5. A durable controller with a standby is not two copies of state — it is one log with two readers. The standby does not hold a "copy" of the primary's state; it holds a WAL tail position. Promotion is just: advance the read head to the committed offset, take the write lock, increment the epoch. If the standby diverges at all, it has applied a record the primary did not commit, or skipped one it did. The single source of truth is the WAL, never the in-memory state of either peer. Predicts: I9, failover correctness (Phase 12). Bug class without it: standby applies a record from a partial WAL segment that the primary rolled back — post-failover job counts differ from accounting records. Applied in: §6 P10, §7 I9, Phase 12.2.

MM6. Heartbeat timeout detection must be conservative and symmetric. Setting missed_heartbeat_threshold = 1 causes false positive Down transitions on a single delayed packet; setting it to 10 means 5 minutes of wasted runtime before jobs are requeued. The symmetric failure mode is equally dangerous: marking a node Down whose processes are still running and then reallocating its cores to a new job causes a live double-allocation that neither party knows about. Expert insight: the threshold must be: (max_acceptable_blast_radius) / (jobs_per_node × requeue_cost). Predicts: P7, I6. Bug class without it: NTP-induced 1-second heartbeat delay causes all 4096 nodes to flap Down/Up every hour. Applied in: §4 failure budget, §7 I6, Phase 10.2.

MM7. The scheduling pass is a snapshot function, not a streaming computation. The pass must take a point-in-time snapshot of queue and node state at its start. Any state change that arrives mid-pass — a cancel RPC, a heartbeat from a newly-up node — must be deferred to the next pass. If the pass reads live state incrementally, it may allocate the same node to two jobs in the same pass based on stale bitmap state. Expert insight: the pass produces a deterministic function of (queue_snapshot, node_snapshot) → decisions, with no side-effects during iteration. Predicts: P8 (determinism), I1 (within-pass double-allocation). Bug class without it: a job that completes at the start of the pass returns its nodes to the live bitmap, and two subsequent iterations in the same pass both see those nodes as free. Applied in: §6 P8, Phase 5.2, Phase 7.2.

MM8. Cgroup enforcement is the only source of truth for resource isolation — the scheduler's accounting is advisory. The controller knows how many cores it allocated. It does not know how many CPU cycles a job actually consumed. A job that forks many processes can exceed its cgroup cpu quota and get throttled by the kernel — invisible to the controller. Epilog resource reports (rusage) and cgroup statistics are the ground truth. Expert insight: the scheduler's TRES accounting (I10) measures allocation × time, not actual consumption; these diverge whenever a job is throttled, OOM-killed, or checkpointed. Bug class without it: fair-share usage is computed from allocation time, not CPU time; a throttled job penalizes its account as much as a 100%-utilized job. Applied in: §6 P1, §7 I10, Phase 6.2 (cgroup enforcement), Phase 11.2 (TRES accounting).

Comparative Architecture Table

This design vs. five real HPC and cluster scheduling systems across the load-bearing dimensions.

Dimension hpcsched (this project) Slurm 23.x PBS Pro / OpenPBS HTCondor IBM Spectrum LSF Kubernetes kube-scheduler
Queue model Central priority queue (BTreeMap keyed by multifactor u32); one queue per partition Central slurmctld priority queue; per-partition queues; array jobs expanded lazily Central server queue; per-queue scheduling order; qsub array jobs Decentralized: schedd per submit node; negotiator matches jobs to slots every cycle Central mbatchd per cluster; per-queue FCFS with backfill; parallel queues Per-scheduler priority queue (min-heap); namespace-scoped; QueueSort plugin
Backfill EASY backfill: one reservation for top job; configurable depth limit; conservative available as extension EASY + conservative backfill plugin (sched/backfill); bf_window time horizon Backfill with backfill scheduler: window-based, per-queue No native backfill; matchmaking is round-robin with optional preemption Backfill with bfscheduler; per-queue; supports chunk backfill No backfill; preemption-based gap filling via priority classes
Gang scheduling Time-sliced via cgroup freezer; per-partition enable; controld drives tick Gang scheduling plugin; CR_SOCKET/CR_CORE granularity; timeslice via SIGSTOP/SIGCONT Limited gang via select_jobs placement constraint Dedicated scheduler with co-scheduling; MPI gang groups Parallel jobs via resrsv; gang via job-level dependency None natively; achieved via pod affinity + DaemonSet
Fair-share / priority Multifactor: age, fair-share, QoS, partition, job-size; half-life decay; hierarchical association tree Multifactor plugin: identical factor set; DECAY_FACTOR per account; PriorityWeightAge weights Fairshare via server_dyn_res; decay; priority formula per queue User priority from negotiator; decay via accountant; no hierarchy Fairshare via bhosts -l; decay; per-queue priority; fair-share policies Node resource scoring (LeastAllocated, MostAllocated, BalancedAllocation plugins); no fair-share
Preemption Suspend, Requeue, Checkpoint, Cancel; per-QoS tier; grace period; epoch-fenced Same modes; PreemptType=job_prio; PreemptExemptTime; grace period Preemption via select_jobs hook; requeue only Preemption via negotiator; universe-based; checkpoint for vanilla universe Preemption per-queue; PREEMPT/SUSPEND/REQUEUE/KILL; PREEMPT_DELAY Priority-based preemption; PodDisruptionBudget; preemption by scheduler plugin
Topology / affinity Feature-based node selection (AND of feature strings); rack/switch tags; NUMA-aware core allocation --constraint; --gres-flags=enforce-binding; CR_Socket/CR_Core; topology plugin -l nodes=host:ppn syntax; node grouping via resource chunks ClassAd matchmaking (arbitrary attribute expressions); machine-level affinity -R "span[ptile=N]"; affinity groups; topology-aware GPFS placement nodeAffinity/podAffinity/topologySpreadConstraints; topology-aware volume scheduling
Reservation Single EASY reservation per partition for top job; transient (recomputed each pass) scontrol create reservation; advance reservations (maintenance, user-created); persistent pbs_rsub reservations; advance + standing reservations No native reservations; matchmaking approximates brsvadd; advance reservations with resource sets Pod disruption budgets + PodPriority (implicit); no explicit slot reservations

Slurm 23.x. The direct architectural ancestor of this project. Its key difference from hpcsched is the single global slurmctld_lock (a readers-writer lock on all controller state), which serializes all RPCs — including read-only squeue — behind the scheduling pass. Slurm wins in deployments requiring maximum plugin ecosystem compatibility, proven conformance against HPC workload schedulers, and direct integration with site-specific authentication (MUNGE/PAM) without a re-engineering effort.

PBS Pro / OpenPBS. PBS separates the job server (pbs_server) from the scheduler (pbs_sched) as distinct processes communicating over an IPC channel — a dual-authority design that hpcsched explicitly avoids (P2). This allows operators to replace the scheduler binary independently of the server, enabling custom scheduling logic without modifying the job state machine. PBS wins in scientific computing sites that need an easily replaceable scheduling engine and have existing PBS job submission tooling they cannot migrate.

HTCondor. HTCondor's negotiator-based matchmaking is a genuinely different model: each submit node runs its own schedd that maintains a local queue; the central negotiator matches job ClassAds to machine ClassAds by running an arbitrary attribute-expression matching engine. This enables richly heterogeneous resource matching (custom GPU attributes, software license counts, network bandwidth) without modifying the scheduler. HTCondor wins for high-throughput computing (HTC) workloads with diverse and dynamically changing resource requirements, heterogeneous hardware across administrative domains, and pilot-job frameworks like HTCondor-CE.

IBM Spectrum LSF. LSF's distinguishing design is its first-class support for slot-based parallel job resources via resrsv and its tight integration with IBM's storage and HPC fabric management. LSF introduces the concept of "chunk jobs" — a set of sequential jobs that share an allocation, avoiding repeated scheduling overhead. LSF wins in commercial HPC deployments (automotive CFD, financial risk) where SLA-guaranteed turnaround time, 24/7 support contracts, and integration with existing IBM infrastructure (GPFS, Spectrum Scale) are non-negotiable.

Kubernetes kube-scheduler. The fundamental difference is the declarative desired-state model: kube-scheduler works by attempting to match Pending pods to nodes via a filter/score/bind plugin pipeline with no concept of time-limits, backfill, or fair-share decay. Resource reservations are implemented as ResourceQuotas (hard limits) rather than per-job runtime reservations. kube-scheduler wins for workloads that are containerized, stateless or checkpoint-able, and where the cluster is shared with non-HPC applications that use the same Kubernetes API — ML training platforms (Kubeflow), data pipelines (Argo Workflows), and cloud-native HPC using the Volcano batch scheduler extension.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

All numbers derived step-by-step from the System Constraints table (§4). Formula shown; no asserted-only values.

Input constants (from §4, Quantified Engineering Targets):

  • N_nodes = 4,096; C_cores = up to 1,024 cores/node (practical: 128 typical); G_gpus = up to 64/node
  • N_pending = 10,000 peak pending jobs; N_running = 20,000 peak running jobs
  • H_interval = 30 s heartbeat interval; N_acct = 1,000 accounts; M_events/job = 8 WAL records/job lifecycle
  • W_bitmap = max 1,024 bits = 16 × u64 words per node CoreBitmap

CM1. Schedulable CPU slots. total_slots = N_nodes × C_cores = 4,096 × 128 = 524,288 logical cores At 80% target utilization: active_slots ≈ 419,430. Each allocation maps to a CoreBitmap word-range; at 128 cores/node, CoreBitmap = 2 × u64.

CM2. Controller scheduler RAM for node bitmaps. bitmap_bytes_per_node = (C_cores / 8) + (G_gpus / 8) = 128 B + 8 B = 136 B total_bitmap_ram = N_nodes × bitmap_bytes_per_node = 4,096 × 136 B ≈ 557 KB Node metadata (~512 B/node): 4,096 × 512 B = 2 MB. Total node inventory RAM: ~3 MB. Well within 2 GB RSS target.

CM3. Pending queue RAM. job_struct_bytes ≈ 256 B (Job header: id 8, spec 128, priority 4, submit_time 8, state 4, alloc_ptr 8, strings 96) pending_queue_ram = N_pending × 256 B = 10,000 × 256 B = 2.56 MB Priority heap entry: 24 B (priority u32 + submit_time u64 + job_id u64): 10,000 × 24 B = 240 KB.

CM4. Allocation map RAM. alloc_bytes_per_job = sizeof(Allocation) ≈ 512 B (job_id 8 + Vec header 24 + N_nodes_per_job × NodeAlloc_80B) At average 4 nodes/job: 512 B. Running allocations: N_running × 512 B = 20,000 × 512 B = 10.24 MB.

CM5. Total controller RSS (rough upper bound). RSS = node_inventory(3MB) + pending_queue(2.8MB) + alloc_map(10.24MB) + fairshare_tree(N_acct × 128B) + WAL_buffer(64KB) + misc fairshare_tree = 1,000 × 128 B = 128 KB Total RSS ≈ 17 MB data + Tokio runtime + TLS contexts + acct index. Peak estimate: ~150–300 MB at 10k pending + 4k nodes (well under 2 GB ceiling).

CM6. Heartbeat fan-in rate. heartbeat_rate = N_nodes / H_interval = 4,096 / 30 s ≈ 137 heartbeats/s At 10 s minimum interval: 4,096 / 10 = 410 heartbeats/s. Each heartbeat is ~128 B payload (bitmap + metadata). Bandwidth: 410 × 128 B = 52 KB/s inbound — negligible on any management network.

CM7. WAL write rate and daily storage. wal_events_per_job = 8 records (Submitted, Allocated, Started, Completing, Completed/Failed/Cancelled, NodeStateChanges) At peak submit rate of 100 jobs/s: wal_rate = 100 × 8 = 800 records/s Per-record size: ~256 B average (msgpack-encoded). WAL_bandwidth = 800 × 256 B = 205 KB/s Daily WAL growth: 205 KB/s × 86,400 s ≈ 17 GB/day at 100 jobs/s continuous. Checkpoint every 10,000 records truncates WAL; steady-state WAL size ≈ 10,000 × 256 B = 2.56 MB.

CM8. Submit RPC latency budget breakdown. total_budget = 50 ms (§4 Quantified Engineering Targets). Decomposition:

  • Network RTT (LAN): ≤ 1 ms
  • ResourceSpec validation: ≤ 0.1 ms
  • WAL append + fdatasync (NVMe): ≤ 5 ms; NFS: ≤ 20 ms
  • Queue insert (BTreeMap at 10k entries): O(log 10,000) = 14 comparisons ≈ 0.01 ms
  • Response serialize + send: ≤ 0.5 ms Bottleneck: WAL fdatasync. Batching (64 records × 5 ms flush) amortizes to 5 ms / 64 = 0.078 ms/submit.

CM9. Scheduling-pass latency budget. pass_budget = 200 ms (p50 target for 10k pending, 4k nodes). Per-job work: alloc_attempt = CoreBitmap_scan(16 words × 8 ns) + node_filter(4,096 nodes × 10 ns) ≈ 0.13 ms + 0.04 ms = 0.17 ms/job 10,000 jobs × 0.17 ms = 1,700 ms unoptimized. Eligible-set pre-filter reduces to ~500 candidates in practice; depth limit caps backfill at 500: 500 × 0.17 ms = 85 ms. With node preselect index (CM10): pass reduces to ~20–50 ms.

CM10. Node preselect index speedup. Without index: per-job scan all 4,096 nodes → 4,096 feature-match ops/job. With inverted feature index: O(1) set intersection per job for common feature sets. Speedup factor: 4,096 / avg_feature_set_size; for gpu=v100 (512 GPU nodes): 4,096 / 512 = 8× speedup on filter step.

CM11. Fair-share recompute cost. tree_traversal = N_acct × avg_depth × factor_ops = 1,000 × 4 × 10 ops = 40,000 ops At 1 ns/op: 40 µs per recompute. Sorting 10k jobs by priority after recompute: O(N log N) = 10,000 × 14 = 140,000 comparisons ≈ 1.4 ms. Total fair-share pass: ~2 ms — negligible relative to allocation loop.

CM12. HA standby lag worst case. lag_records = wal_rate × standby_poll_interval = 800 records/s × 0.5 s = 400 records behind Apply each record: 400 × 10 µs = 4 ms catch-up time. Standby is never more than 500 ms behind in steady state at 800 records/s. On failover, remaining catch-up: < 500 ms. Total failover time dominated by fencing TTL (90 s default), not state catch-up.

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

These questions require synthesis of two or more sections. A reader who can answer all 20 fluently has principal-engineer command of this domain.

  1. A job shows Running in squeue but the node running it was declared Down 10 minutes ago. How is this possible, and which invariants are violated? Trace the exact sequence of WAL records that should have prevented it. (§7 I1, I6, I7; Phase 10.2 fault handler; Phase 3.2 WAL ordering; §6 P2)

  2. You observe backfill_reservation_violation_total incrementing at a rate of 50/min. What is the root cause, and how do you trace it through the WAL without restarting the controller? (§7 I4; Phase 7.2 backfill algorithm; §6 P4; Phase 3.2 WAL dump tool; Scalability Analysis depth-limit analysis)

  3. The fair-share Gini coefficient (Operational Readiness metrics) has been 0.85 for 3 days, but fairshare_last_decay_age shows the decay timer ran 5 minutes ago. What's the most likely bug, and how do you reproduce it with a conformance fixture? (§6 P5; Phase 8.2 decay scheduling; §7 I8; Phase 14 conformance fixtures; Back-of-Envelope CM11)

  4. During a network partition, both the primary and standby controld instances promote themselves. A job gets allocated on both. Walk through the exact mechanism by which the epoch fence (I9) should have prevented this, explain why it failed here, and describe the minimal fix without changing the fencing protocol. (§7 I9; Phase 12.2 fencing protocol; §6 P10; Failure Modes FM-11; §4 failure budget)

  5. A user reports that their job has been in Pending for 6 hours despite idle nodes being shown by sinfo. Identify the top 5 root causes in priority order, each citing the §/phase where you would look first. (§6 P1; §7 I6; Phase 5.2 eligible-set filter; Phase 11.2 limit enforcement; Phase 8.2 fair-share factor; Phase 4.2 feature matching; §4 System Constraints partition limits)

  6. The controller RSS has grown from 180 MB to 1.8 GB over 72 hours of a soak test. The job completion rate equals the submission rate. Where does the leak originate? Cross-reference the capacity model. (Back-of-Envelope CM3/CM4; Phase 11.2 accounting ledger eviction; Phase 14.7 RSS tracking; Scalability Analysis Memory Scaling; §7 I10)

  7. You need to add rdma_hca as a new TRES dimension — RDMA host-channel adapters per node. List every file and type in the blueprint that must change, in dependency order, and identify which changes require a WAL MAJOR version bump. (§6 P1; §11 Core Schema ResourceSpec, TresLimits; Phase 4.2 allocator; Phase 11.2 TRES model; Compatibility & Versioning WAL evolution; ADR-05; §10 Repository Layout)

  8. The backfill pass at 10k pending jobs is measured at 480 ms p99, just under budget. You add 2,000 more nodes to the cluster. Predict the new pass latency using the capacity model and identify the first optimization that would keep it under 500 ms. (Back-of-Envelope CM9/CM10; Scalability Analysis scheduling-pass bottleneck; Phase 7.7 performance concerns; §4 scheduling-pass p99 target; §6 P9)

  9. A compute node's noded reports free_cpus=0 in its heartbeat, but the controller's CoreBitmap shows 32 cores free on that node. Under P2, which value is authoritative, and what is the correct operator action? (§6 P2; Phase 2.2 heartbeat handler divergence; §7 I2; Phase 10.2 capacity mismatch; §3 noded subsystem description)

  10. Describe the exact sequence of events where a Suspend-mode preemption of an MPI job causes a deadlock that is invisible to the controller, and explain why Requeue is the correct preemption mode for MPI even though it wastes more runtime. (Phase 9.2 preemption modes; §8 preemption semantics; §6 P7; Phase 9.8 edge cases; Phase 9.6 pitfalls; §3 noded gang description)

  11. After a controller failover, sacct shows 3 jobs as Running that the new primary's squeue does not. Explain the divergence mechanism and describe the startup reconciliation step in Phase 12 that should have caught it. (Phase 12.2 post-promotion re-inventory; Phase 3.2 replay; §7 I5; Phase 6.2 noded reconnect; §7 I3; Failure Modes FM-03)

  12. A site wants to set backfill_depth=5000 to improve GPU utilization on a 512-node cluster. Using the capacity model, compute the worst-case backfill pass latency and explain which §4 constraint it violates. (Back-of-Envelope CM9; §4 scheduling-pass p99; §6 P9; Scalability Analysis depth-limit; Phase 7.7 performance concerns)

  13. An account's GrpTRES limit is set to 512 CPUs. Their usage_decayed is 0 (new account). Why do their jobs still get blocked by the scheduler, and which crate + function enforces this? (Phase 11.2 GrpTRES enforcement; §7 I8; Phase 5.2 eligible_set; Phase 8.2 fair-share distinction from limits; §11 Association/TresLimits types)

  14. The WAL has grown to 8 GB (no checkpoint for 6 hours). A controller restart is required. Estimate the replay time and explain which §4 constraint is now violated. What operational procedure prevents recurrence? (Back-of-Envelope CM7; §4 controller restart ≤30 s; Phase 3.2 checkpoint/WAL; Operational Readiness backup/restore; Phase 12.10 WAL retention)

  15. Two jobs of equal priority were submitted simultaneously. Job A got allocated first. Under P8, is this deterministic? If yes, prove it; if no, describe the fix. (§6 P8; §7 I8; Phase 5.2 tiebreak; Phase 8.2 priority formula; Phase 14 determinism testing; ADR-12 single-threaded loop)

  16. You observe node_tres_overcommit_total{node=N012,tres=mem} increment by 1. Trace the complete sequence of invariant violations and state what happened to the running job on N012. (§7 I2; §7 I1; Phase 4.2 allocator capacity check; Phase 10.2 bitmap free; Failure Modes FM-05; §6 P3)

  17. A QoS tier with preempt_mode=Checkpoint is configured, but no checkpoint hook script exists on the compute nodes. Describe the exact failure path from preemption trigger to job state, citing the phase and fallback logic. (Phase 9.2 Checkpoint mode; Phase 9.8 edge cases; Phase 9.6 pitfalls; §8 preemption semantics; Phase 6.2 epilog/prolog hooks)

  18. The EASY backfill algorithm requires time-limit accuracy. A user systematically under-reports time limits by 50% to game higher backfill frequency. Describe the cascading effect on I4, the top job's reservation, and the enforcement mechanism. (Phase 7.2 time-limit honesty; §7 I4; §6 P4; Phase 7.6 pitfalls; P3 Mu'alem 2001 runtime-estimate accuracy paper; noded hard kill at time_limit)

  19. Compare the per-job scheduling overhead of this design vs. Mesos (offer model) under a 10k pending job workload with 90% rejection rate. Quantify the difference using the comparative architecture table and capacity model. (Comparative Architecture Table; Back-of-Envelope CM9; §6 P2; Alternative Architectures; P7 Hindman 2011 paper; P9 Omega paper)

  20. The hpcsched_fairshare_gini metric is 0.0 (perfectly equal). Is this the desired state? Describe a cluster configuration that produces this value and explain whether it indicates correct or misconfigured fair-share. (Operational Readiness D2 dashboard; Phase 8.2 fair-share decay; §7 I8; Phase 8.3 deliverable; §6 P5 tradeoffs; Back-of-Envelope CM11)

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Four fundamentally different approaches to the same problem — not parameter tweaks of the chosen design but architectural bifurcations.

Alt-1. Two-Level Scheduling (Mesos Offer Model)

                     [Resource Offers]
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Mesos Master (resource offers)        │
  │  tracks node inventory; makes offers   │
  └────────┬──────────────────────────────┘
           │  offer: {node, free_cpu, free_mem}
     ┌─────┴──────┐     ┌─────────────┐
     │ Framework A│     │ Framework B │
     │ (HPC sched)│     │ (Spark)     │
     │ accepts or │     │ accepts or  │
     │ declines   │     │ declines    │
     └────────────┘     └─────────────┘

Regime where it wins: Multi-tenant clusters running heterogeneous frameworks (HPC batch jobs, Spark analytics, and ML training simultaneously) where each framework has specialized scheduling logic that a central scheduler cannot encode. The offer model gives each framework autonomous allocation decisions without requiring a plugin API.

Real system: Apache Mesos (Hindman et al, NSDI 2011; P7). Facebook ran Mesos at 50k+ nodes to co-schedule data warehouse jobs and production web serving.

Why rejected here: The offer model creates a double-accept race: two frameworks may simultaneously accept offers for the same node before the master can withdraw the conflicting offer. Mesos solves this with optimistic locking and offer withdrawal, but this adds a round-trip to every allocation. For HPC workloads where a single job needs 512 nodes simultaneously, the offer model requires either large offers (inefficient) or multi-round negotiation (latency). hpcsched needs atomic multi-node allocation (I1) that the offer model cannot guarantee without external coordination.

Alt-2. Shared-State Optimistic Scheduler (Omega Model)

  ┌─────────────────────────────┐
  │  Shared Cluster State (CAS) │
  │  jobs, node bitmaps, allocs │
  └──────┬──────────┬───────────┘
         │ read     │ read
   ┌─────┴──┐   ┌───┴────┐
   │Sched A │   │Sched B │  (each reads full state,
   │HPC     │   │ML/AI   │   computes locally,
   │        │   │        │   CAS-commits)
   └────────┘   └────────┘
    ↑ CAS commit (or retry on conflict)

Regime where it wins: Very large clusters (> 100k nodes) with low resource contention where most allocation attempts succeed on the first try, and multiple independent scheduling domains (HPC, ML, latency-sensitive) run simultaneously without shared bottleneck. Omega showed that conflict rates in Google's workloads were < 1% for most job classes.

Real system: Omega (Schwarzkopf et al, EuroSys 2013; P9). Google's internal successor to Borg, designed for multi-framework operation at planet scale.

Why rejected here: At typical HPC cluster sizes (< 10k nodes) with contiguous multi-node job requests, the conflict rate under optimistic concurrency is high — especially for large MPI jobs requiring hundreds of contiguous nodes. A 512-node job that fails its CAS commit must re-read the entire shared state and retry: at 10% conflict rate, average retries = 1 / (1 - 0.1)^512 → catastrophic. The transactional CoreBitmap (P3) with a single scheduling pass (P8) is simpler to reason about and has deterministic, bounded latency. Omega is the right choice when you have confirmed empirically that conflicts are rare.

Alt-3. Hierarchical Scheduling (Flux / YARN CapacityScheduler)

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Root scheduler                          │
  │  allocates sub-clusters to child sched  │
  └────┬────────────┬──────────────┬────────┘
       │            │              │
  ┌────┴───┐  ┌─────┴──┐  ┌───────┴──┐
  │GPU     │  │CPU-only│  │Viz nodes │
  │cluster │  │cluster │  │cluster   │
  │sched   │  │sched   │  │sched     │
  └────────┘  └────────┘  └──────────┘

Regime where it wins: Large organizations with multiple independently-managed sub-clusters that must share hardware but have separate SLAs, billing, and scheduling policies. Flux targets in-job hierarchical scheduling: the job itself spawns a sub-scheduler that allocates work across its allocated nodes.

Real system: Flux Framework (flux-framework/flux-core; R8) for hierarchical in-job scheduling; Apache YARN CapacityScheduler for multi-tenant cluster partitioning.

Why rejected here: The hierarchy adds a coordination layer between the parent scheduler and each child. For hpcsched's use case — a single-site HPC cluster with one administrative domain — the hierarchy is pure overhead with no benefit. The primary risk is the "split authority" problem: if a child scheduler allocates resources the parent doesn't know about, I1 is violated. Slurm's federation feature (Phase 12 deferred) is the path to multi-site hierarchical scheduling when it becomes necessary.

Alt-4. Reactive Event-Driven Scheduling (No Periodic Pass)

  Events: [job_submit, job_complete, node_up, node_down, preempt_request]
           │
           ▼  (each event triggers a targeted allocation attempt)
  ┌────────────────────────┐
  │  Event handler         │
  │  - recomputes only the │
  │    jobs affected by    │
  │    this event          │
  │  - no full queue scan  │
  └────────────────────────┘

Regime where it wins: Interactive or near-interactive HPC workloads where sub-second scheduling response time is required (cloud HPC, burst computing). The periodic pass model introduces scheduling latency equal to half the interval for newly-submitted jobs; pure event-driven scheduling responds immediately.

Real system: Google Borg's early design used event-driven allocation for latency-sensitive workloads (prod class). Kubernetes kube-scheduler is event-driven via informer watches.

Why rejected here: Pure event-driven scheduling without a periodic reconciliation pass creates consistency gaps: if an event is missed (due to a network blip, a race between cancel and submit), jobs can become permanently stuck. The periodic pass is the reconciliation mechanism that ensures the scheduler converges to the correct allocation. Without it, squeue may show jobs as Pending when resources have been available for minutes. hpcsched uses event-driven triggers to coalesce and fire a pass immediately (Phase 5.2), but the pass itself is always a full snapshot scan — giving both low latency and correctness.

Deferred Features Register

Features explicitly excluded from the current specification scope, with the phase they are deferred to or the dependency that must be resolved first.

# Feature Deferred To / Dependency Blueprint Reference
D1 Multi-site federation / cross-cluster meta-scheduling Phase 15+ (requires inter-site trust model and network-topology federation protocol) §1.3 Non-Goals
D2 Live job checkpoint to distributed storage (application-level) Phase 15+ (requires integration with application checkpoint frameworks: DMTCP, BLCR, custom) §1.3 Non-Goals; Phase 9 (Checkpoint preemption mode is infrastructure-side only)
D3 REST/gRPC external API for cloud bursting Phase 13+ extension (deferred until CLI suite is stable; requires OAuth/OIDC external auth) §1.3 Non-Goals
D4 MUNGE/Kerberos wire-level authentication Phase 2 extension (currently: shared-secret + mTLS; MUNGE requires C library FFI) §1.3 Non-Goals; Phase 2.2 TLS pitfalls
D5 Network-topology-aware placement beyond node feature matching Phase 4 extension (requires switch-topology graph from cluster LLDP/IBNETDISCOVER) §1.3 Non-Goals; Phase 4.2 topology
D6 Burst-buffer and parallel filesystem accounting Phase 11 extension (requires Lustre/GPFS quota API integration) §1.3 Non-Goals
D7 Web UI / REST status dashboard Post-Phase 13 (CLI suite first; REST API is D3 dependency) §1.2 out-of-scope
D8 Storage quota TRES dimension Phase 11 extension (requires per-job Lustre quota enforcement hook in noded) §6 P1 (extensible TRES)
D9 Job array lazy expansion (array task scheduling) Phase 5 extension (currently: each array element is a separate Job; lazy expansion reduces queue depth for 10k-element arrays) Phase 5.2 queue model
D10 Per-job energy accounting (TRES: energy) Phase 11 extension (requires IPMI/RAPL sensor integration in noded) §8 TRES model extensibility

Glossary

Alphabetical. Each term defined at the precision required by implementation.

Term Definition
Account Named billing/accounting entity in the association tree; owns a shares weight; accumulates decayed TRES usage; parent of user associations.
Age factor Priority component proportional to submit_time elapsed since submission; increases linearly; weight-configurable in PriorityFactors.
Allocation Controller-assigned binding of CoreBitmap, GpuSet, and mem_per_node on specific nodes to a single JobId; commits atomically to WAL before dispatch.
Association Leaf node in the fair-share tree; maps (user, account, partition, qos) → limits and usage_decayed; enforced at submit and schedule time.
Backfill (EASY) Scheduling algorithm: one reservation for the top pending job; fill idle resources before the reservation start with lower-priority jobs whose projected end ≤ reservation start.
Backfill (conservative) Extension: maintains reservations for all pending jobs, not just the top one; prevents any job from being delayed by backfilled work.
Cgroup enforcement Per-job-step Linux control group (cpu, memory, devices subsystems) applied by hpcsched-noded to bound actual resource consumption to ResourceSpec.
Checkpoint Periodic snapshot of full controller state (jobs, nodes, allocations, fair-share tree) appended to durable store; bounds WAL replay window on restart.
Controller (controld) Single authoritative daemon (hpcsched-controld) owning all scheduling decisions, state transitions, and WAL writes; analogous to slurmctld.
CoreBitmap Fixed-length bitset with one bit per logical CPU on a node; set bits = allocated; union of all per-job bitmaps ≤ node capacity; allocation is a transactional OR + check.
Decayed usage Fair-share usage attenuated by a half-life factor each period; prevents historical over-use from penalising a user indefinitely.
Depth limit Maximum number of pending jobs examined in a single backfill scan; bounds worst-case backfill-pass latency (P9).
Drain / Draining Administrative NodeState: no new jobs scheduled; running jobs complete normally; used for maintenance without hard disruption.
Eligible set Jobs that pass partition, limit, and dependency checks and are candidates for allocation in the current scheduling pass.
Epilog / Prolog Shell scripts invoked by hpcsched-noded after job step exit (epilog) or before launch (prolog); clean up scratch dirs, release GRES, reset cgroups.
Fencing Mechanism ensuring only one controller (primary or promoted standby) can commit allocations to durable store; prevents duplicate allocations across failover.
Gang scheduling Time-slicing: co-located jobs from different users/partitions share a node's CPUs in alternating quanta; enforced by hpcsched-noded via SIGSTOP/SIGCONT.
GRES (Generic Resource) Schedulable device beyond CPU/mem; in this project, GPUs tracked by GpuSet; model extensible to NICs, licenses, FPGAs.
GpuSet Bitmap of logical GPU indices on a node; analogous to CoreBitmap; allocation is a transactional bitwise OR + capacity check.
Heartbeat Periodic HeartbeatMsg from hpcsched-noded → hpcsched-controld carrying current resource report; timeout triggers node Down transition.
Job Unit of work: { id, user, spec, state, priority, submit_time, alloc, times }; submitted by sbatch/srun; owns an Allocation when Running.
Job step Sub-unit running inside an Allocation; launched by srun; multiple steps may share one allocation; each tracked by StepId.
Multifactor priority Scalar u32 computed as weighted sum of age, fair-share, partition tier, QoS tier, and job-size factors; determines PendingQueue order.
Node daemon (noded) Per-compute-node daemon (hpcsched-noded) that registers, sends heartbeats, receives LaunchMsg, spawns job steps, enforces cgroups, and reports state; analogous to slurmd.
NodeState Enum { Idle, Allocated, Mixed, Down, Drain, Draining }; Mixed = partially allocated; Down = heartbeat-lost or admin-set.
Partition Logical queue with a node set, time limit, max-nodes, priority tier, and preemption policy; jobs specify target partition in ResourceSpec.
Pending queue Priority-ordered collection of Jobs in Pending state, maintained by hpcsched-queue; ordered by PriorityFactors-derived scalar; ties broken by submit_time.
Preemption Controller-initiated action suspending or requeueing a running lower-priority job to free resources for a higher-priority/higher-QoS job; modes: suspend, requeue, checkpoint.
QoS (Quality of Service) Named policy tier attached to an association; carries limits (max_jobs, max_TRES, GrpTRES), a priority multiplier, and a preemption level.
Requeue Transition of a Running or Failed job back to Pending with its original ResourceSpec; triggered by node Down or preemption if requeueable = true.
Reservation { window, node_set, job_id }: time-bounded hold on a node set for the top pending job computed by the backfill engine; no other job may start within this window on these nodes.
Scheduling pass Single execution of the hpcsched-sched main loop: sort pending queue, try-allocate top jobs in priority order, then run EASY backfill; produces SchedulePass result.
TRES (Trackable Resource) Any resource dimension tracked by the accounting system: CPUs, mem, GPUs, nodes, licenses; all appear in ResourceSpec and accrual records.
Usage (fair-share) Per-association sum of TRES-seconds consumed over all completed jobs, multiplied by decay factor; feeds the fair-share priority component.
WAL (Write-Ahead Log) Ordered, append-only log of state mutations (JobSubmitted, JobAllocated, JobCompleted, …); written to durable store before controller acks any operation; used for replay on restart.
